DOMESTIC SERVICE AND SWEATED LABOUR.

Sir,—ln answer to the letter signed "Mary Ann," I would like to inform "Mary Ann" and many others that there is a Domestic Servants' Union, and that the secretary's address is Miss G. Stephens, 28, Raebury-street, Glasgow, N.B. There are several branches formed now. Both servants and mistresses would find such a union beneficial, instead of the present registry system. LABOUR WOMAN.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOVERNMENT POLICY.

Sir,—l am sure no one will be proud of the state of affairs now existent in Ireland. For months past your columns have been flooded with diatribes on the wrongs of Ireland, but nothing has been said of the past hundred and fourteen years of beneficent and ameliorating legislation, or of the millions spent in further- ing the objects of that legislation. Our present Government have seemed to be bent on out- Heroding Herod, and, since they succeeded in muzzling the House of Lords and voting their docile henchmen a salary of ..t4OO a year, they have gone from one extravagance to another, re- gardless of the result and of the ultimate day of reckoning. I see not a shadow of real Liberal- ism in anything they have done; they have created a dictatorship and have postponed to the Greek Ka Bends the old Liberal war cry of "Peace, retrenchment and reform." Peace— where is it ?—with Irishmen arming and drill- ing against each other. Retrenchment—when the Budget stands at two hundred millions! Reform—can anyone tell me where I may find it? Shades of Palmerston, Peel and Gladstone! What would they say if they were here? What about the present arming in Ireland? Th, tion of Ulster is intelligent as being a (It nation to resist oppression, but what is meant by the National Volunteers? Are they to revel the despotic yoke on free Ulster, and is the Government going to support them with the national forces? The Ulstermen's action is justified on the ground of loyalty and self-defence. The action of the Nationalists is not justified for the simple reason that no one is likely to attack them; armed Nationalism is a threat of coercion to- wards Ulster. If the Government and National- ism is well disposed towards Ulster, steps should be taken to grant free and unrestricted autonomy to the Ulster province, and that pro- vince, at some future time, when it saw and realised the benefits under Home Rule, would ask to be included in a united Ireland. Our Goveimment and its supporters have sown the wind, and now the natural consequential crop is maturing for the harvest. PHIL SQUOD.

Die lerthi

Tijurril Arims.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

Mr. J. Hunsworth presided at the weekly meeting of Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday afternoon. The speaker was Mr. Travers, of Widnes, who dealt with the historic building of the walls of Jerusalem by Nehemiah. Favoured by the King and passionately fond of the Holy City, he carried through the work in record time, and his considerate treatment of the workers made them love him. He was a God-fearing man, as were many of the present-day leaders of the working class. Messrs. Healey and Yearsley also took part in the service.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

Sabbath School Anniversary Services.

The Sabbath school anniversary services were held at St. John's, on Sunday, the preacher being the Rev. J. G. Tosh, Rochdale, moderator of Manchester Presbytery, of which Warrington forms part. In addition to conducting the usual forenoon and evening services, Mr. Tosh delivered an interesting and appropriate address to the scholars in the afternoon. There was b, large attendance in the evening, when the preacher chose as his text passages from the first and third chapters of 11. Timothy, in which the Apostle Paul refers to the Chris- tian parentage and upbringing of Timothy. Mr. Tosh said the letters of Paul to the young minister, Timothy, were amongst the most pe were es on dealing lhi ne gh d them t ever wrote,helpe andth e passages u i understand stahey n a whehybecTaimmoethjar mcaanme. m It waasn made eidpiaand plain what kind of home he had been born into, What was the,character of his parents and forebears, and the quality and nature of the religious training which he had had from babyhood. When they had been told that about a man they had been told a great deal. The home and family influence must ever be the most important, though not the only one. Continuing, the preacher called attention to the use of the Scriptures in Timothyis home, and the effect upon him of the Christian home influ- ences as he grew up- to manhood. It was a beautiful picture said Mr. Tosh) of a Christian home, and was not the only one, but typical of many. And where could they better get those religious influences than in the inviolate sanc- tity of home life. What more natural than for Christian parents to see that their children might be before all else children of God? The fairest vineyard inside the Church was that for the cultivation of their young life. A most bright and pleasing feature of the services Was the singing of children's hymns by a choir of Sabbath school boys and girls, and the rendering of two solos, "My Saviour " and "Peace, the gift of God's love," by Miss Ethel Hardcastle, of Stockton Heath, one of the senior scholars, who was accompanied on the piano by Mrs. J. Snailham. The musical part of the services was under the care of Mr. A. G. Lewsey, organist.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
CANCER AND SUICIDE.

Wife's Discovery in a Bedroom,

"Oh, God, Ernie, what have you done?" was what Mrs. Edith Crutchley, of 32, Factory-lane, asked her invalid husband, who lay on the floor with an open razor in his hand. The story of the man's sufferings and his suicide was related at the inquest conducted by Coroner Brighouse at Warrington on Wednesday. Mrs. Crutchley said her husband was 35 years of age and a general labourer. He had been in failing health for a number of years, and about four years ago he underwent an operation for an ulcer. For the 'past five months he had been suffering from cancer, and had been attended by Drs. Lees and Liston. On the previous morning she gave him some medicine, and after he had been downstairs some little time she assisted him upstairs and told him to sit down whilst she went to fetch something from down- stairs. On her return to the front bedroom she missed him, and she called to him. He replied, from the back bedroom, "I am here,Edie," and when she saw him there in such a position, she made the remark quoted above, to which he replied, "God forgive, I did not know." The razor dropped from his hand, and although doctors were called he died shortly afterwards.

A Very Sad Case. The Coroner: It is a very sad case. He was suffering from this terrible disease, and in all probability he would not have got better. Pos- sibly his sufferings had unhinged his mind and made him say to himself, "I don't care what happens." I think you, the jury, are justified in saying he was of unsound mind at the time. A verdict of " Suicide whilst temporarily in- sane" was returned. The Coroner expressed the sympathy of the jury and himself with the widow.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 4, 1914.

LEITERS BY AN IRISHMAN.

Survey of the Facts Governing the

Demand for Home Rule.

V.—IRELAND UNDER Tad UNION

(Continua).

It is not too much to say that hatred of English rule has been steadily growing since the Union, and Grattan's prophecy has been fulfilled: " What you trample on in Ireland will sting you in America." The Irish race, pour- ing out in fever-stridan shiploads across the Atlantic, carried with them, into the New World, hatred of the Power which had op- pressed them in the Old.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "CORPSE" REVIVED.

The rebellion of 1848 was easily crushed, out, and Cavan Duffy, one of its leaders, left his country a few years later—" leaving her," he said, "stretched like a corpse on a dissecting table." He went to Australia, where he helped to found a representative Government, and be- came the Prime Minister of Victoria. But in his absence " the corpse" revived. In 1867 the Fenian organisation spread over the whole of Ireland and, officered by Irishmen who had gained experience in the Independence War, created widespread terror. It failed in its mili- tary purpose, but the Irish Church Disestab- lishment was directly due to its power and organisation. Gladstone's Land Act of 1870 was the outcome of its agitation, and though cer- tain English legislators have attempted to remedy the existing evils in Ireland, they have always failed, because they preferred their own ideals and methods to those of the Irish people. Parnell's policy aimed at two things. First, general land purchase, under which the occupier of the land would be made the owner of it, as had been done years before in Prussia, by State intervention. Secondly, he demanded Home Rule. Both objects were treated by the British Government as chimerical. As to the.land reform, Mr. Gladstone, by his Act of 1881, established the principle of dual ownership, which secured the Irish tenant against the arbitrary raising of his rent, and against arbitrary eviction, and to that extent laid the foundation of settled prosperity in Ire- land. But the machinery was costly and cum- brous, and it saddled every farm in Ireland with a constant series of law suits. Mr. Glad- stone himself soon saw this, and in 1886, along with his Home Rule Bill, he proposed a land purchase measure, pledging one hundred mil- lions of British credit. The idea was scouted, and was, indeed, the principal means of wreck- ing his proposals. Yet in 1903 the Unionists finally adopted Par- nelPs policy. The Irish leader's plan was only tried after 25 years of agrarian war, which had embittered the whole relation between landlord and tenant, and which had driven, half the gentry out of Ireland.

NO IRISH SWING OF THE PENDULUM. Home Rule, which both English parties laughed to scorn under Butt, and in the early days of Parnell, was in 1886 adopted by one of the two great English parties. The agitation to secure it is not yet at an end, but the struggle has entered a new phase. In 1885, for the first time, an extended franchise was established in Ireland. Ever since Irishmen got the vote on a more democratic scale, four-fifths of the repre- sentation of Ireland has been returned pledged to Home Rule. There is no swing of the pendu- lum in Irish politics. Save in about half a dozen constituencies there has been no change since 1885. At the present the whole of Ireland practically is in favour of Home Rule—even Ulster returns 17 out of 33 members in its favour. It can hardly be said that there is an agitation for Home Rule in Ireland; there is simply a steady affirmation of a demand. But there is an agitation against Home Rule in Eng- land, and the nature of it is noticeable in an appeal to sectarian prejudice and to religious I hate.

RELIGIOUS PREJUDICE. The English Government has succeeded only in one object in Ireland. It has succeeded in in- creasing the divisions between religions since the Union. With the five years before the Union, a very strong agitation was started by Protestants in Ireland for giving full equality to Catholics. The "United Irishmen's Society" was founded by Protestants. Since the Union the majority of the leaders of Irish public opinion have been Protestants. How the names run through my mind: Emmet, Smith O'Brien, John Mitchell, John Martin, Isaac Butt, Shaw, Parnell, Swift Mac Neill, and others, but the mask of Protestants have been detached by Eng- lish influences from their fellow countrymen. This was done by maintaining the principle of Protestant ascendency. Irish Protestants were made to feel that so long as the Union continued they would he 'a privileged class; that they would have the whole government of the country and enjoy all its pre- ferment. When the Disestablishment of the Church was proposed, Irish Protestants threat- ened civil war, as to-day they threaten—when Home Rule becomes law. The maintenance of the Union meant keeping a grip on the spoils of office. Yet whenever this Protestant ascendency was threatened in its citadel—they wept to their English brethren. The direct effect, then, of English rule in Ire- land has been to foster religious bigotry and in- tolerance; and the whole activity of the Union- ist party and the Unionist Press at the present moment is devoted to persuading Irish Protest- ants that they must regard Irish Catholics as their enemies and to persuade English Protest- ants that they must regard Irish Catholics as the hereditary enemies of England and the 'de- termined oppressors of their Protestant fellow countrymen. This is fudge, and I shall deal with the matter in a later article.

VI.--HOW THE UNION STANDS TO-DAY. To sum up, then, for the last 40 years, Eng- land has attempted a good deal of remedial legislation for Ireland; but it has always been undertaken in the wrong spirit—redress has only been attempted after violent agitation has taken the place of argument; and in the wrong method, as is proved by the fact that each successive Land Act has needed repeated amendment. The one measure, whose success is admitted on all sides, has been that universally recommended by Irish statesmen—namely, the establishment of a peasant-proprietorship and occupying ownership.

Furthe-r, the main character of English rule has been the maintenance and encouragement of religious divisions, and the concentration of all political administrative power in the 'hands of one favoured denomination. . The administration of justice has been dis- credited by the constant employment of coer- cion, and the practice of packing juries by chal- lengirA all men on the panel, except persons whose known predispositions enabled the prose- cat JOE to count on them for a verdict. The success of the government may be judged by the fact that the population has during the last half century steadily dwindled, until, within fifty years, it has been reduced by four millions—a fact without parallel in modern Europe. Add to this that the amount of income assess- able to income tax has remained stationary since 1895, whereas in Great Britain it has in- creased by £300,000,000. It is evident, then. that the Act of Union shows no very splendid results.

RECENT BENEFICENT CONCESSIONS.

At the same time there are indications that the decline of Irish prosperity has terminated; the trade of the country is improving. The opponents of Home Rule point to this fact as a reason why the Union should be maintained. The answer is that the improvement in the state of Ireland is due, not to the Unionist policy, but to the policy which the Unionists opposed at every turn. The Unionist party-op- posed at every turn the land legislation of the G.O.M.—Mr. Gladstone. They were driven to extend its principles by violent agitation, which was treated as a crime. The Land Act of 1903 undoubtedly, a beneficent measure—was the direct outcome of an agitation in 1902, for which a dozen Irish members of Parliament, with numberless lesser individuals, were condemned to terms of imprisonment. The concession of local government was, in the first place, a concession which, under the Act of Union, could not be denied if there was to be union. When local self-government was ' given to England, it could not logically be re- fused to Ireland, although it was withheld for several years. and was at last conceded with the hope of "killing Home Rule by kindness." Another important reform recently granted

was the establishment of a University suited to the needs of Irish Catholics, under Mr. Birrell's Act. This is a valuable change; but Ireland had to wait fifty years for it, and generation after generation of young Irishmen grew up without the advantage of a University educa- tion—an Irish Parliament would have settled the matter years before. Nor has the concession of local government, of a University, or land purchase, by any means exhausted the legislative needs of Ireland.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER PRESSING NEEDS.

_ There is agreement among the Irish of all classes and creeds that the system of primary education and of secondary education is insuffi- ciently endowed and badly administered; the English Parliament has not the time to attend to these matters, and if it had, it would prob., ably settle them in accordance, not with Irish, but with English opinion. For instance, inter- mediate education was given the "whisky money" as its revenue; with the result that the endowment of secondary schools declined from ' ,t 70,000 to £40,000, and finally to .R. 15,000 because Irishmen drank less whisky. If an Irish: ad- ministration had devised this plan, the English Press would have been very witty over it. is snd a Vice-Regal Commission Perfect agreement that the Irish poor law system is disgracefully costly andAg Again, thereet, reported, many years ago, as to the lines of reform, but Parliament has had no time to attend to this matter, and there is no prospect that Parliament will have time to attend,tTh The Irtsh railway system, an intereWirital to the prosperity of the country, is admittedly in urgent need of ieform. Again, a Commission has reported and made recommendations, but there is no prospect whatever of Parliament •attending to these recommendations. To those who are further desirous of obtain- ing more proof of the incompetence of British Government, both in administration and legis- lation of Ireland, I can but recommend Mr. , Stephen Gwynn's recent article in "The Nine- teenth Century." Complete failure is therein proved.

A CENTURY OF BAD GOVERNMENT. It cannot be denied that England, in the hundred years since the Union, has governed, and is governing, Ireland badly. I do not know that this is denied; it is admitted by English statesmen of both parties. Take, for example, this utterance of Lord Salisbury in 1865 : " What is the reason Hutt a people with so bountiful a soil, with such enormous resources as the Irish„ lag so far behind the English in the race? Some say that it is to be found in the character of the Celtic race, but I look to France and I see a Celtic race there going for- ward in the path of prosperity ith most rapid strides—l believe at the present- moment more rapidly than England herself. Some peopre say that it is to be found in the Roman Catholic religion; but I look to Belgium and there I see a people second to none in Europe, except the English, for industry; singularly prosperous, considering the small space of country that they occupy, having improved to the utmost the natural resources of that country, but "distin- guished among all peoples of Europe • for the earnestness and intensitY of their Roman Catholic belief. Therefore, I cannot say that the cause of the Irish distress is to be found in the Roman Catholic religion. . . . It cannot be demagogues, Romanism, or the Celtic Race. What then is it? I am afraid that the one thing which has been peculiar to Ireland has been the Government of England." No answer to the argument in this passage is given by pointing out ;that Lord Salisbury, in youth and age alike was opposed to Home Rule. It has never endeared the Government of Eng- land to Ireland that those who condemn the system still advocate its continuance.

CROSFIELD'S BOY SCOOTS. Workmates and Playmates Compete

for Priieo.

The great training ground of many of tne boys of the rising generation is the Boy Scout movement, and its adoption at Messrs. Joseph Crosfield's large works, which has resulted in the formation of the. 2nd Warrington (Cros- field's Own) Troop, }the been attended with en- couraging success. Its inception made work- mates into playmates, and the good feeling that has arisen thereby was evidenced on Wednes- day evening, when, at the works recreation ground at Sankey the Scouts held the first of what is to be an annual sports festival. Each item was keenly contested. Mr. and Mrs. John Crosfield were present—the latter to hand over the prizes.

, THE EVENTS. Probably the greatest enthusiasm was focussed on the mile flat handicap, when the limit man was an easy first and the scratch man a comfortable second. From the start of the race Bibby drew attention by his fine running. The little fellow possesses a remarkable stride, and with easy running he had gained consider- ably when the second lap was reached. Although he lost a little ground later, there was never doubt as to his being the victor. Barton, the scratch man, ran very well, but only took the second place a few yards from the tape. In the 100 yards race there was little difference between the first two, who ran very well. The year's championship was won by Barton, who secured four first places and one second place; lie thor- oughly deserved his honour.

Presentations to the Winner's. Prior to the distribution of the awards several final events were decided in the presence of Mr. and Mrs. John Crosfield and other well- known personages connected with the firm, and later, following the presentations, Captain Lay- ton called for three cheers for Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield. The response was hearty, and Mr. Crosfield, in replying on behalf of his wife, said be could assure the boys that it was with great pleasure that she was present to distribute the awards, and thereby show her interest in the Scout movement, and especially in " Crosfield's Own." He paid a tribute to the officers and others re- sponsible, and stated they had received a com- munication from the Scout authorities, which stated the desire to place the movement on a sounder basis. What pleased them most was to learn that the firm was described as being, with other firms, pioneers in the Scout movement. After again ex Scoutmaster and assistants, he said their work pressing his indebtedness to the would have been of no avail if the boys had not come forward. The training they got would ' benefit them all through their lives, and after expressing the wish that the company would in- crease numerically, he hoped the time would come when no boy in their works would fail to pass through the companY• (Loud applause.) Mrs. Crosfield carried a beautiful bouquet, which was presented to her by Scout Orford on behalf of the troop.

'WARRINGTON'S CAPTURE. T. Laws, oiWhines, Joins the AA ilders- pool Ranks.

'terero,

A VERY usEruL BACK. FZIII Jur had been busy during the early part of the week that T. Laws, of the Widnes Club, had signed on for Warrington, but it was not until Wednesday night that he actually did so. He is fairly well known to Wilderspool sup- porters, although when he last played against the Warrington team at Warrington he did not seem to do quite justice to himself. Laws, towards the end of last season, had some disagreement with the Widnes Committee, and, coming to Warrington, he played with Rylands' Rees. in a few of their latter matches and did excellent work. He is now employed at Messrs. Ilylands' works in Church-street. - The pew player may be considered as an im- portant capture by the Warrington Committee. Oldham and a few other clubs were after .him when it became known that lie was on the transfer list, so it says a good deal for Warring- ton that they have succeeded in securing - the prize. Laws is considered one of the most ver- satile players Widnes has ever possessed. He is young and active, with plenty of dash in his play, and can perform good work either at half- back or amongst the threequarters. Generally, however, he is considered most serviceable on the wing threequarter, where his speed can .he put to the best use.

RUNCORN N.U. CLUB.

At the annual meeting on Tuesday of the Runcorn N.U. Club :the directors' repoct con- tained all appeal to the shareholders and players to put all their efforts in one direction, with the object of bettering the position .of the club. The Works' Competition had again been success- ful from a financial point of view, but the Schoolboy Competition had proved a failure financially, and they were of opinion that unless the interest revived somewhat they very snusly considered the advisability of dropping the :.ompetition for a time.

There was an increase of nearly .620 on season tickets at £lO2, and gate receipts were up by over .6150. The profit and loss account on June 4th, 1912. showed a debit balance of .00 is. sd. This ha& been turned into a credit balance of .623 Os. 6d. They had also a scheme under con- sideration for the development of the land now lying idle, and, when completed, they proposed calling a meeting of the shareholders to consider it. The report was adopted.

Struggles With Violent Week-end

CROWD STONE THE OFFICERS.

Stories of policemen's struggles with violent prisoners Were unfolded at the local Police Court on Saturday and Monday, and in one case it was stated that the attitude of the crowd be- came so threatening that stones were thrown at the officers,

===== 0002601_19140704_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS 61st APPEARANCE.

On Saturday morning, before Messrs. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair), A. J. Jolley and T. Pemberton,' and Alderman D. Tinnion, Patrick Richard Moran, described by the police as a loiterer of no fixed abode, was charged with being drunk and disorderly on the previous day, and with assaulting P.C. Joynson, of the Che- shire Lines Railway Police. The officer stated he wasson duty on the Cen- tral Station, when he saw the prisoner in a drunken conditim and acting in a very dis- orderly manner. SPrioner was taken into ells_ tody, and at his request the officer released his held, but upon reaching the station steps pris- oner slipped away amongst the people and was again caught in Horsemarket-street. Moran be- came verrviolent, and on the way to the Police Station lie said if he had a knife he would " stick it through" the officer Prisoner threw himself on the floor and owing to his violence Sergt. Woodland had to go to the railway con- stable's assistance. Supt. Williamson stated that they had to take prisoner's boots off at the Police Station to pre- vent him doing injury. Accused stated that when they were in-Legh- street the officer tripped him, "just to see if I ' would 'slip' into him." The officer picked him out of a number on the platform, and said noth- ing to the others. He had been asked to carry a box on to the platform. The Chief Constable said that prisoner would have taken the box on to the platform if he had received no instructions at all. "This man," said Mr. Nicholls, "is one of the finest tale- tellers in South-West Lancashire, and he is keeping up his reputation this morning." Moran had been before the Court 60 times previously. Prisoner said he had had no drink for over four hours before the time in question. The Chief Constable: It was not your fault. Prisoner was committed to prison for three months. .

===== 0002601_19140704_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THREW AWAY THE HANDCUFFS.

The two other cases were heard on Monday, before Messrs. S. Wright (in the chair), G. Pill- ing, W. Roberts, W. Furness and T. Locker. Aldermen W. Bolton and James -Evans, when Thomas Kelly, Inicklayer's labourer, 8, Dol- man's-lane, was narged with being drunk and disorderly in Dolman's-lane on Saturday; with assaulting Anthony Gilmore, and further with assaulting P.C. Harrison. Gilmore said he was a labourer and resided at 5, Dolman's-lane. Shortly after eleven o'clock on Saturday night he was in the house, when prisoner came in, and without any provocation threatened witness and challenged him to fight, but before the witness had time to defend-him- self prisoner knocked him down and. kicked him on the head. The officer came on the scene and arrested prisoner. When in Rylands-street Kelly became very wild, and when the officer tried to resort to the "snaps" prisoner -snatched them away. and threw them -under a tramcar. The man was very violent with the officer. P.C. Harrison said he saw the prisoner in a very drunken condition. Ve was stripped to the waist and was striking right and left at the last witness. Kelly butted witness several times, and it was with great difficiilty he was got to the Police Station. Prisoner said Gilmore and his wife were doing all they could to injure him. He had received his papers to attend an annual reservist train- ing, and if he did not go he would be the loser by about .t 5. Gilmore hail done all he could to get him away from those lodgings. The Chief Constable said that was the only lodging-house- in the town that would have him. " He is a reptile,". remarked the Chief. Prisoner: "Forgive you for saying that. I am not a snake !" The Chief Constable said prisoner had a bad record, and as a result of his conduct his wife had left him, and as a consequence tile children were stranded in the town.: Prisoner was committed to prison for two months, and as he was leaving the dock he was muttering something about "reptile."

• .iRISH .VERSUS ENGLISH. Another story of assault was heard when James O'Neill or Riley, forge labourer, 39, Pier- point-street, was charged with using obscene language in Bewsey-street on Saturday, and also with assaulting P.C. Moody in. Brown- street. P.C. Green stated that at ii o'clock on Satur- day night he was in Bewsey-street, accompanied by P.C. Moody, when they saw a crowd of 'people. He heard prisoner using ,bad language. They asked him for his name, but as he gave several different ones, he was taken into custody. When in Brown-street prisoner kicked P.C. Moody's legs from underneath him, and incited the crowd to release him. shouting, "Come, let's sho'w the English what the Irish can do." P.C. Moody corroborated. The crowd, he said. became very threatening, and when near the Police Station the police were stoned. As a re- sult of his struggle with the prisoner his legs were bruised and his trousers were completely spoiled. Prisoner was fined 55. 6d., with the alterna- tive of a month.

FARM AND GARDEN,

DECREASING RURAL POPULATIONS.

Cheshire Among the Remarkable Exceptions.

Rural Population and Work. The Registrar-General finds that there are only eight persons to the 100 acres for all West- morland. The North Riding of Yorkshire in its agricultural parts has only ten persons to the 100 acres, adding in the non-agricultural area only eleven. The unfortunate thing about these figures is that it is not a case of waste land waiting to be taken up, but of agriculture fail- ing to pay a dividend in certain cases. In Northumberland fifty years ago there were 99 agricultural labourers where now there are 84, in Westmorland there were 38 where there are now 35. In Wales the thinness of population is not greatest in the mountains, but in Radnor, where there are only six agricultural persons to every 100 acres. This is perhaps the most typical of our unprosperous agricultural coun- ties, and it includes some that ail famous for this or that special crop: Unfortunately this fact an scarcely hide from us the conclusion that the other parts must be doing very ill indeed. In the agricultural regions : Cornwall now has 19 persons to every 100 acres; Devon, 14; Dorset, 16; Wiltshire, 17; Huntingdon, 15; Lincolnshire, 15; Leicester, 18; Rutland, 18; Monmouth, 11; Hereford, 14; Shropshire, 16; Notts, 17; West Riding, 16; Lancashire, 18; East Riding, 13; Durham, 11; Cumberland, 11. Here we have eighteen counties gravely under- populated on the rural side. Regions where population is declining at the rate of five per cent, or more in ten years are Helston, Truro, Liskeard, and St. Germans in Cornwall, Credi- ton in Devon, Taunton in Somerset, Cricklade in Wilts, Faversham in Kent, Elverston in Lan- cashire, Barnard Castle in Durham, Penrith and Longtown in Cumberland, and Wortley in York- shire.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Large Absolute Decline.

The number of those engaged in agriculture skows a decline of 25,000 or over in fifty years in Cornwall, Devon, Somerset, Wilts, Suffolk, Nor- folk and Lincoln. If we included the Welsh counties we should find relative stability in North Wales, but a disastrous decline in the middle counties and also in the south. Owing to their small area Huntingdon, Dorset, Bed- ford, Rutland and Hereford do not come into the above class, but they would do so if they were of average size. Agriculture has picked up in the West Riding of Yorkshire, but supports a reduced population in the other two Ridings. The figures for the Lake District are discourag- ing. Some remarkable exceptions are Cheshire, Hampshire, Surrey, Worcester and Stafford, where those nourished by agriculture are an increasing band. Essex, despite the famous importation of Scottish talent, has lost nine points in 171. It is most curious to note these divergencies. We may assign Worcester's im- provement to fruit-growing, and that of Che- shire to its dairying, but why Hampshire should do better than Dorset is hard to say, and the good figares for Stafford are unaccounted for, unless it has done like Cheshire, and increased its numbers of farmers and these are engaged in supplying the great industrial regions with Milk and cheese.—" Standard." PROTECTION FROM FLIES.

During the summer months flies are often a nuisance to horses. Though it may not be pos- sible to keep a stable entirely free from flies, much can be done to prevent them from get- ting too numerous, and to reduce the annoyance and discomfort which they cause. Among the methods of prevention are:—

_ Create a draught, by arranging windows and doors, so that a draught can be produced. Place wire-gauze over the window openings. Remove all dung immediately. Disinfectant solutions, made up either of carbolic acid or of creolin, when Sprinkled •nn the walls and the floor of the s.a hie, are very useful.

Horses may he protected to a certain extent against the attacks of flies by a linen cr cottcin .sheet, which is also useful in preventing dust from settling on the horse's coat. , Horses with docked tails are. at a great dis- advantage. The mane is also a means ef defence, but a shortly-trimmed mane is tif little use for the purpose, while in the case of a hogged mane the neck has no protection. Ear-nets may suitably be employed in the case of horses whose skin is very sensitive to the attacks of flies.

Horses that are turned out to grass very often suffer great discomfort from flies in the, months of July and August, especially when they are docked and when their mane is very short and hogged. Nothing much can be done to protect turned-out horses from the attacks of flies, as the application of disinfectant dressings is only of very temporary effect in deterring the flies from settling on the horse. The horses can, of course, be taken into the stable during the heat of day, in order to prevent them being worried by the flies in the open, but it is not, as a rule, practicable to adopt this plan on account of the trouble and time which it involves.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Plant Dahlias, stake, and tie. If there is room the old tubers may also be put out. Plant out Tuberous Begonias, watering and mulching immediately after planting. The long growths of Violas may he pegged down, which will give a far neater appearance to the beds.

Hardy Ferns will be benefited by Soakings of water, if they are growing in positions where they do not receive their share of rain.

- To keep away slugs, dust rotind Gladioli with powdered lime.

. - Syringe Roses often with a solution of Quassia or Soft Soap. It is only by persistently spray- ing that the trees can be kept clean. Give Border Carnations a dusting. of ,Super- phosphate of Lime, and water it in.

_ _ • Some perennials are liable to get too tall, especially if grown close-to a sunny wall: The rampant growth may be curtailed by pinching out the growing points at the present time. Side growths will then be induced to push out, which will supply flower stalks in due course.

VEGETABLES.

Pinch out the tops of Broad Beans and syringe with Quassia or Soft Soap. The shortening of the leading shoot will help the podding and keep the black fly- in check. Thin out Beet, Carrots, Onions, &c. • Open the soil with the hoe alongside all crpps. To realise the full value of the Broad Bean, the pCals should he gathered when not more than half-grown. This early gathering enables the later Pods to grow to a larger size. Syringe each day and give a good soaking of water.'

• Syringe Runner Beans often. Sow mote rows. floe the soil frequently. Runner ,Beans- work out r‘ather, expensive when several rows are re- quited, and the following 'method, which does away with, sticks, is recommended :—Sow in double rows, and when the plants are 15 inches high pinch out the points. Side growths will follow, which must also be stopped when six inches long, after which allow them to grow. Put a few short twig sticks along the rows, which will be all the support required. Make another sowing of Parsley for winter use. Given some protection during bad wintry weather the supply may be prolonged. The gardener's best friends are the hoe and a soot bag. If you hoe between growing crops and dust the plants once a week with soot, all sorts of pests will be kept in check.

TROUBLED IN MIND. Motorman Ends His Lite In Tragic

Manner. COUNER AND THE "POOR BOX."

The fact that he had run over a child, although at the inquest he was exonerated from all blame, greatly troubled William Baxendale, Corporation motorman, 7, Rosewood-avenue, and this was not his only trouble, for after taking ammonia poison he made the following state- ment to his wife : " There will be some bother about a woman. Her husband is going to get a separation order." He died in the Infirmary on Friday, and Mr. S. Brighouse conducted the inquest on Monday, when Mr. T. S. ,Steel watched the proceedings for the relatives.

The widow said her husband was 31 years of age and was employed by the Warrington Cor- poration. After interviewing a magistrate with respect to a vaccination exemption, he returned to the house by the back door, appearing very strange in his manner. There was blood on his mouth, and when she asked him what was the matter he said he had taken ammonia and made the above statement. When she asked him why, he said he had drunk the poison in Pad- gate fields, and had thrown the bottle away. He was conveyed to the Infirmary in Dr. Sutherland's •motor car, after the doctor had administered emetics.

A STRANGE STORY. The Coroner: Was this the first intimation you had had that .there was likely to be some trouble between your husband and another woman?—Yes.

You had heard nothing beforeP—No. You did not know whether it was true or not? —No.

Did it seem to upset your husband?—l never noticed anything different than usual. Of course, he would not let me see anything. Did it trouble him when I held an inquest on a child he had run over ?-oh, yes. But it was said he was not to blame?—Yes.

You had no idea he would take awa-3;.his-ii7e? —Certainly not. Dr. Sutherland said there was no doubt the man died from ammonia poison. The Coroner: Did he make any statement about trouble ?—He said the cause of the trouble was with the woman, and rather than face the trouble he had taken the ammonia.

NOT A COURT OF MORALS.

The Coroner (to the jury) : This is not a Court of morals. We have only to find out how the than came by his death r Whether he had any trouble with this woman or not it is not for us to investigate, and there is not much satis- faction in investigating it no's' that the poor fellow is dead. Some people, when they have trouble, would rather take their lives than face it. This man's wife knew, nothing about the trouble, and rather than face it he took the poison. A verdict of "Suicide whilst temporarily insane!' was returned. In answer to the Coroner, Mrs. Basendale said she had one child five years old and another three months old, and she had no money. BROTHERHOOD. All Denominations Join Rands for , Local Forward Movement. NATIONAL REVIVAL CAMPAIGN.

There were signs of the barriers of sectarian- ism being at last broken down in Warrington at the second meeting of the National Revival Campaigners, which took place in the Wycliffe Memorial Buildings on' Monday evening. The Rev. T. T. James presided, and amongst a repre- sentative assembly to consider the scheme for interesting every man in town in church work, there were present the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis), the Rev. Father Wilson, the Revs. W. Bracecamp, J. M. Buckmaster, Wm. Reid and A. J. Chessum, whilst letters of apology were reCeived from the Revs. B. Percival, W. A. Beckett, E. Morgan and Jas. Davies, Mr. C. E. Parker and others. Having alluded to the noble objects of the campaign, the CHAIRMAN said it was impossible for any campaign like that proposed to be con- fined to any particular organisation. The only , way it was possible to make 'a Christian appeal to the men of the town was to include every de- nomination, none of whom need sacrifice their beliefs in joining their ranks. (Applause.) Mr. B. FARRAR, secretary, said that the sub- committee recommended that the meeting should decide to "work up" the town in wards, and appoint a committee for each to organise meet- ings. The Rev. W. BRACECAMP said they were all agreed that this was a great campaign. The more one looked at it the more difficult it be- came. He hoped the leaders of the Church of England, Roman Catholics, Nonconformists, Adult Schools and Brotherhoods would each see how much they could do, and then co-operate. (Applause.)

THE RECTOR'S APPRECIATION. The RECTOR wished to express his apprecia- tion of the desire to make the town more re- ligious. He would be very glad indeed to call together the clergy of the town and district to see how far they could assist. (Applause.) 'UTTER GODLESSNESS." The Rev. Father WILSON thought the business rather premature for him to say anything definite about it, but it was quite possible for them to work together against the common enemy of utter Godlessness. One could not, of course, hope for any good to come out of this if there was anything in the shape of proselytism. He could not speak like the Rector had done, since he was "not a rural dean or any other sort of a dean." (Laughter.) The Roman Catholics had five parishes only, and they had a sort of campaign every few years, when invita- tions to attend the church were sent out by means of circulars and house-to-house visits. They might in time come to join this with the National Campaign. (Hear, hear.) The Rev, W. BRACECAMP then proposed that a secretary be appointed for every ward in the town. This was seconded, supported by the ' Revs. WM. REID and J. M. BUCKMASTER, and car- ried. Mr. REID moved that a sub-committee be ap- pointed to choose the ward secretaries, and after discussion the following sub-committee was elected: The Revs. T. T. James and W. Brace- camp, Messrs. Cox (Adult Schools), Holland (Wesleyans), George Dale (Working Men's Mis- sion), J. Ellison (Ellesmere-street), Councillor Shaw (Brick-street), and Mr. B. Farrar (Wycliffe P.S.A.). The Rev. T. T. James acts as chairman and Mr. B. Farrar as secretary to the central com- mittee. which deals with finance and superin- tends the work generally. This committee will consist of two representatives from every ward committee, and the ward committees will be elected at the meetings called by the ward secre- taries. Before the meeting closed reference was made to the fact that the meeting was an historic one, as there had never been held before in Warrington a meeting representative of every form of religion like that one.

WORKHOUSE MINISTER.

The Rev. H. Ogden Suggested for the Position. HONOURS FOR OFFICIALS.

The Nonconformists in the Workhouse are now to have services conducted by a Nonconformist pastor. This is the outcome of an agitation started some months ago, and as a result the Guardians a month ago decided to make such an appointment. The Rev. 11. Ogden, minister of Golborne-street Baptist Church, has been nominated as the new Nonconformist chaplain. At the fortnightly meeting of the Guardians, held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding, the House Visiting Committee reported that the Warrington Free Church Council had notified the Clerk of the nomina- tion of the Rev. H. Ogden, of the Baptist Church, Golborne-street, as Nonconformist minister to the Workhouse, and it was recom- mended that Mr. Dale, Mr. Garnett, and the Clerk meet the Rev. H. Ogden upon a suitable date, and discuss the details of the proposed appointment with him, reporting in due course to this committee.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

The Treatment of Cyclists by the Railway Companies.

TO SAVE PETROL.

, I have been wondering recently when I shall have to give up my car, for the cost of petrol is so great that I am sure it is only those who are wealthy who can afford to pay the price. I have heard rumours of petrol at lid. per gallon, and many other statements, but they do not seem to me to materialise. I have been trying what economy will do with regard to the petrol bill, and I find that I can save a considerable amount by exercising care in one or two directions. In the first place, I never allow the engine to run while the car is stationary, even if it is only for two or three minutes. At one time I was willing to let the engine run for ten minutes or more, but I have come to the conclusion that a car eats up petrol under such conditions, and that no advantage is gained. In the old days, when cars were hard to start, there was some excuse, but now there is none. Opening the throttle slowly, instead of rapidly, will save a certain amount, as will attention to the flood- ing of the carburettor. I find it is not necessary to waste petrol in this way, since filling is suffi- cient. These may appear small points, but they , have saved me something during the past weeks.

A Dual Engine. Unfortunately makers of motor engines have i never succeeded in the past n making an engine of high efficiency which possesses the same degree of flexibility and sweetness of running as in that of a low efficiency motor. Attempts have been made to strike a happy medium, but as a general rule manufacturers have concentrated all their skill in the direction of turning our engines belonging to one or the other class, and with splendid results. The high efficiency engine is rather too harsh in running for a number of motorists, but, on the other hand, the motor possessing only low efficiency Is somewhat slug- gish. An article in "The Autocar," by Mr. W. G. Watson, however, explains the working of a variable cam engine, which can be used for both purposes. Either type of engine can be had at will by simply bringing a different set of cams into play; therefore a maximum speed or a sweet running car can be had. Such an engine has been constructed, and if it proves the suc- cess that is expected of it there is little doubt but that the principle will be adopted generally, much to the advantage of all car users.

Cycles and Railway Companies. I wonder if railway companies will ever waken to the fact that they are public servants and that they have a duty to perform. There has been a considerable amount of agitation in the past with reference to the treatment of cyclists when taking their machines with them, but there appears to be little chance of any good results arising therefrom. There is a possi- bility that something may be done in the future, however, since Mr. S. R. Noble, the seoretary of the National Cyclists' Union, has been giving evidence before the Royal Commission on Rail- ways that is now sitting. In the course of his evidence he pointed out that the two avowed objects of the union are to obtain a better scale of charges for cycles and to secure greater care in their transport. It certainly seems unfair that a third-class passenger should be allowed 100th. of luggage free, whereas a cyclist w perhaps, his machine as his only piece Of I gage, should have to pay extra. The cl weighs considerably less than the amount granted to the ordinary passenger, and as the owner usually handles it himself there is not even the excuse of additional labour.

Cycle Oils. Practically every cyclist will tell you that one of the greatest needs at the present day is for a better form of cycle lubricant. The average lubricating oil is generally too thin and it runs out of the working parts, and collects dirt and dust with the utmost ease. One of the worst oils in this respect is that which is sold for burning and lubricating. This I have always found to be of very little use for either purpose, and it is very much better to obtain different oils for the lamp and the working parts. What is really wanted is a good thick oil that will not run out after a short period has elapsed, but will remain in the hub, pedals and the variable gear. Naturally a different preparation is needed for different parts of the machine. For instance, the chain, especially if not covered with a gear caSe, should not be treated with oil, but with another mixture altogether. I have found both the following very suitable for this purpose: A mixture of vaseline and graphite or beeswax and graphite. Both will last a long time and keep the chain in good running order. Accidents and Carelessness. I was reading in a contemporary recently a wt eal it happened-writtenaccsoomunet yoefaras ago.road -iiatcihnagripeacnceiddenatt h the finish of the race, and a trap had drawn up to the side of the road, when a racer appeared going for all he was worth, with his head down. Everyone realised that he was making straight for the back of the trap, but their united shouts did not reach him, at least in the desired way, and the result was that the impact was so great that his head went right through the wood- work. Needless to say, he was killed on the spot. In track racing there have been other accidents of a similar nature, but, moreover, the same has been noted on the road. There are a number of people who, when cycling uphill, bend over their machines in such a way that it is impossible for them to see where they are going. Only a few days ago I saw an accident of this kind in one of the roads leading from the Embankment to Fleet-street. The rider was exerting himself to get up the slight hill, and, not looking where he was going, he collided with a newspaper motor van that was backing, into its stable. No one could hold the driver responsible, for the blame could only be attached to the cyclist. Fortunately little damage was done as the speed of neither was great.

Heavy Motors and the Rates. On Wednesday, 24th June, a deputation from the County Councils' Association of England, Wales and Scotland, and the Urban and Rural District Councils' Associations waited on the President -of the Local Government Board to iusrtgreattihvee raemgaentialtmiecansto,iftitihreeglaawrd atnd ehetlaiveyanadmetienv- traffic: and traction engines. One of the princi- pal amendments was that an adequate annual licence fee should be paid on heavy motor cars, the amount realised to be distributed among road authorities on a basis to be arranged. The same suggestion was made with regard to trac- tion engines. The deputation also brought. for- ward the plea that all trackless trolleys or motor omnibuses should be liable to a contribu- tion for the maintenance of the roads over which they run, and, moreover, that 'power be given to the road authorities to regulate the running of such vehicles over their roads.

THE CYCLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

Hill Climbing Roundabout Warrington.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
"A GLORIOUS COAST."

(Continued in next column.)

coFFEIN

The CCC

"To make Coffee in Take a teaspoonful of THOS. SYMING

One of the compensations to a cyclist of a life in smoky Warrington is the ease with which all his homeward rides finish. From whatever quarter he returns a long down-hill of several miles awaits him. This is also a matter of re- proach from old seasoned hands, who have been used to the hills of Lancashire or Yorkshire.

"A lowland cyclist," saith the scoffer, " know- eth not the joy of climbing hills," such as Black- stone Edge or Buckhaw Brow, Sutton Bank or Garrowby Hill. Well, here is a spot close to Warrington where hill roads are plentiful. As a rule, our local cyclist and motorist only sees the long white ribbon of Helsby main road as he rushes through the village on his way to Ches- ter. But by turning upwards behind the new Council Schools, a bit of country awaits him such as cannot be seen nearer than the Peck- forton Hills, or, perhaps, Denbigh. As we climb up between Helsby Hill on one side and Foxhill on the other, the valley becomes very fine, but a good deal of walking will be needed before the top is reached near the Sanatoria. At Alvan- ley Vicarhge the left turn is taken and for several miles the qbality of the atmosphere will explain why this upland moor was chosen for the convalescent homes of the patients of Liver- pool and Manchester Hospitals. If the right- hand road is taken at the letter-box, the Liver- pool Home is passed on the left, and a capital run ends in the glorious coast down Rangers- bank into Delamere Forest. The left-hand road from the corner where the leteer-box stands takes us still higher to the top of Birch Hill, 500 feet above our starting point. Following the direction posts to Kingsley, the Manchester or Crossley Sanatorium stands on our right. and the descent begins. The hill is steep and would make a fine coast but for the visual hair- pin corner at the bottom, which needs a good brake and much care. Kingsley is soon reached, and the return can be made via Acton Bridge or by Pickering Bridge and Preston Brook.

WHO INVENTED THE BICYCLE? The few lines last week concerning the origin of the bicycle reminds me of a topic Gild his often come up for discussion where wheelmen assemble. The invention of the rear drive is usually attributed to Mr. J. K. Starley, of Rover fame, who died several years ago, and to whom the credit was given again in hundreds or news- paper pages. But the honour properly belongs to Kirkpatrick Macmillan, a native of Dum- friesshire, who, in 1842, rode from his home to Glasgow, 130 miles, in a two-days' ride', on a two-wheeled machine driven with cranks and pedals, placed in practically the. same position as in Starley's safety bicycle in the 'eighties. The motion was communicated to the rear wheel with a chain, and a copy of the original machine (Macmillan thought too little of his wonderful invention to take so much care of it) is to be seen in Dumfries-Museum. Many similar machines were made and sold at .-t,7 each, but the sport Lslid,nctt catch on till the-Prenchman, Michaeux, invented the " bone-shaker " in the 'sixties, a machine driven direct by the front wheel, and the forerunner of the "good old ordinary."

CLUB RUNS.

St. George's Cycling Club.—Our ride last Saturday was to Knutsford, and despite the threatening weather a good number turned out, and a very enjoyable day was spent. The coun- try looked splendid, so much so in fact, that it was the cause of a mishap, fortunately only slight. One member, with horticultural pro- clivities. was so carried away with the splen- dour of one garden that he attempted to per- form an acrobatic feat over the hedges in order to get a somewhat better view. After paitaking of tea, advantage was taken of Lord Egerton of Tatton's generosity in throwing ifen Tatt,on Park to the public. The return journey was uneventful. Warrington Wesleyans.—Thirty-one members rode to Davenham and took tea as usual on the lawn at Mrs. Booth's. The return was made via Northwich and Marston, where much inter- est was displayed in the scene of the recent sub- sidences, and Arley, where a very pleasant half- hour was spent in song. An unfortunate skid disabled one lady at Whitley, and she did wisely in not continuing the ride. I would like to deal more fully next week with this class of accident. TO-DAY. Warrington C.C.—Beeston Towers (Ladies' Day). Warrington Wesleyan.—Eaton Hall. Sankey Bridges Wesley Guild.—Boylake.

LAMP TIME. To-day, 9.40; next Saturday, 9.35. SILVER-STREET.

CYCLING AND MOTORlNG—(continued).

RE,aders will remember that a few weeks ago we mentioned that the Middlesex County Council proposed to levy a rate on the 'buses using the new road that it is proposed to construct be- tween Chiswick and ,the Bath-road. The con- tribution in this case was to be !ths.of a penny per mile.

Mr. Samuel's Reply. In his reply, Mr. Samuel stated ttat he real- ised that the time had come when this question should be re-considered. The road authorities Were expected to adapt themselves to the special requirements of the present day, and it was true that the owners of heavy vehicles should be willing to adapt theonselvEs to the requirements of the Road Boards. He proposed that the matter should be considered by a Technical Committee, both as regards the regulations and the amendments necessary in the _existing laws. The position was not satisfactory: Where a service was run by a company not requiring statutory powers, nothing was payable. R. ‘':W. WOODS, THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 4, 1914. SOUTHPORT 6: WARRINGTON. OPEN SPECIAL PURCHASE FOR SALE. mmu 720 COLOURED CREPE BLOUSES All one Price I/101, worth 2/11. 5, MARKET PLACE (Next Door to Post Office). CHAMBERLAIN. WALK ROUND AND INSPECT OUR GOODS. , A great figure and a powerful •

personality has gone from our midst. It may be too early even to guess at the place history will assign to that brilliant son of the capital of the Midlands, Mr. JOSEPH CHAMBERLAIN, but it may safely be foretold that he will rank amongst the foremost of the renowned statesmen who Aised the Victorian era in many respects to a position, politically, second to none in our annals. To us, who can only look upon events as we see them pass before our eyes, and whose scope is therefore limited, we see the picture of a man with tremendous- natural gifts, who strove with his full strength for his country's good. Some of us, it is true, think that there was a fatal turning point in his career, fatal alike to himself, his ideals, and the country he spent himself in serving. In his early Republican and Radical days he said things that would be spoken very harshly of if', say, Mr. LLOYD GEORGE, said them to-

day. He it was Who spoke of the ransom the possessors of wealth ought to pay to the com- munity that allowed them to possess it ; he it was who applied the words of the Scriptures to the drones of Society, whom he likened to the lilies of the field that neither toil nor spin in re- turn for their glorious raiment. He it was who startled his QUEEN by the extravagance— judged by the standard of the times—of his language. He advocated all kinds of reforms that we hold dear, including old-age pensions. The only difference between him and Mr, LLOYD GEORGE IS that Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has been able to accom- plish much that Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN suggested, including even the taking of ransom, which we sometimes call by the name of super-tax. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN fell away, joined the "gentlemen men of England," became in- volved in a long and wasting war, and started a campaign against Free Trade, which has had a bad time Of it. Still even

- here his intentions were right.

lie thought to build up a great Imperial ideal, but he went to work, we think, in the wrong way. Liberals believe they have a better method, which time Will prove. But the greatness of the man was revealed when he collapsed under a strain that was too great for him to bear, and the party to which he had attached himself was left desti- tute of a single really great man. What the Liberal party lost by his secession may, in some degree, be gauged if we try to think what a driving force he and Mr. LLOYD GEORGE would have made had they remained in one and the same camp. But ifs and regrets are now in vain. Along with the whole of Eng- land and the Empire we express our profound sorrow at the passing of one in whom the Englishman's love of freedom burned with a fierce and un- quenchable light, and who indi- cated, at all events, the course along which we should go to achieve it.

Bargains in Pianos.

DAWSON'S, 59, Sankey Street, Warrington.

NO CIVIL WAR. There is to be no civil war in Ireland. The Lords, according to present indi- cations, will pass the Amending Bill through the second reading, and, as LORD LANSDOWNE has intimated, will then concentrate upon making provi- sion for the exclusion of Ulster. As in the case of the Parliament Act, and the revolutionary Budget, 'which was the forerunner of that Act, the Tory back- woodsmen, known as the "Die-bards," are restive under the acknowledgment of their leaders that there are occasions when further resistance would be Suicidal. They are for acting in a given way, and damning the conse- quences pnce again. But these ,reck- less politicians will-be swept aside, and their demise, whether it be hard or easy, will once ,more take place. At a time when LORD LANSDOWNE, as the official head of the Opposition, has indicated in the most practical way that he has paid heed to LORD MORLEY'S statement that he could "hardly think that the House of Lords will have any hand at all in driving Irish Nationalist feeling back again into the old chan- nels of violence .and disloyalty," Liberals will not be disposed to join in a dispute as to which side is capitulat- ing. No doubt both sides will be wil- ling lo do much to. ensure peace where there is a great amount of unnecessary excitement and suspicion, and if a settlement can be arranged we may well leave it there, trusting to time and comm On-sense to allow Home Rule for Ireland to evolve naturally into what it ought to be.

BIRTHS. MARRIAGES. DEATHS.

WALKING DAY.

Yesterday was the saddest day for twenty years from the child's point of view. Leaden skies, which came to us with the dawn, poured out drenching rain during the hours the annual procession usually takes place, and those who ventured forth were more brave than discreet. The Boman Catholics, boldly and wisely, abandoned their procession, and they live to walk another day. By noon the rain had vanished, and the Clerk of the Weather did his best for the rest of the day to atone for the scurvy trick he had played us in the morning.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN PIERPOINT.

Alderman William Pierpoint, who has been honoured in a special degree by the Liberal party this week, as a mark of their appreciation of forty years of service in the cause of Progress, is not quite the father of the Council. He has been in the Council 28 years, and con- tinuouslysince 1894; while Alderman Bolton in the Council 31 years, and continu- ously 1882. Both these gentlemen were hasked beensinceto be Mayor in 1903—one a Liberal and the other a Conservative. Alderman Pierpoint - declined the honour. The respect in which he is held by the party to which he belongs was demonstrated with unmistakable emphasis on Wednesday night at the Liberal Club, and there is little wonder he did not trust himself to reply. That duty was well performed by his son, Mr. Robert Pierpoint.

A UNIQUE RECORD. As will be seen by a paragraph in our local intelligence, three Warringtonians have had conferred upon them this week the degree of M.D. That is a one-week record for a town the size of Warrington that will need some beating. It would be noted by those who carefully read the examination results of the Lancashire and Cheshire Union of Institutes that We pub- lished last week, that among many other dis- tinctions Harold E. Brownbill took the first place in the Union in the second year of tW preliminary commercial course; that John S. Jenks took the second Place in the Union in arithmetic (junior); that Leonard Doncaster took third place in the Union for commercial English and business methods; and that Fred Jones took second place in the Union for wood- carving. This is the class of distinction that brings credit to the town through its educational institutions and its students.

CANCER AND RADIUM. We are 'glad to say that the Manchester radiutn fund is showing marked signs of progress; but More important than that is the accumulation of evidence to prove that radium is a real hope in what had conic to be regarded as a hopeless branch of medical and surgical work. The most striking addition to this evidence is the letter of Professor Thorburn, of Manchester, who, in sending a cheque for ten guineas to the _fund the "Evening Chronicle"

has opened, writes : • One of the saddest, perhaps the saddest, duties of a surgeon in active practice is the daily recurring necessity of telling people who are suffering from cancer that they have passed the stage at which surgery can be of any service, and that nothing remains for them but a linger- ing and a painful death. It is incredible to me, coming into daily con- tact with cases which require its use, that the city of Manchester should not at once provide an efficient supply. and I again tender you my personal thanks for the assistance that you are giving to those of us who are endeavouring to relieve the sufferers from the most distressing and painful of all diseases.

IRISH PICNICS. Our readers know the opinion we hold of LA) deputations that are visiting Ireland "to study the Irish question on the spot." We have exposed the little.'devic,e to deceive more than once, and think. it 711gileSS to-go on slaying the slain. But the trick is being played on other parts of the country, and it is interesting to see the impression it is making. As we quoted last week but one, the "Manchester Guardian" calls it "a stale trick" that imposes on no- body, but injures Conservatism by presenting it to the undeceived, spectator as a party with "any amount of Money that is needed, and not much scruple." The "Daily Chronicle" points out regarding the " reports " of the deputations:— In many cases the very words seem to have been drafted for them to sign; for the "reports" of different deputations, hailing from different' parts of England and professedly quite uncon- nected, contrive to resemble each other not I only sentence for sentence, but often word for word, and point incontestably to a common source.

It is the same old story. They go "to study the Irish question on the spot"; and they re- solve in seven days, or seven minutes, that "Home Rule would be a blunder and a crime." But even where the deputations write their own " report," they can only report on the carefully selected sights that those who paid for their trip let them see. The method is simple. They are taken to Dublin, shown the worst slums, and told that the Corporation is Nationalist, and that is why the slums are so bad. Then they are taken to Belfast, shown its more pros- perous sides, and told that the Corporation is Unionist, and that is why it is prosperous. .. . . The public has an awkward knack of exhibiting more sense than the Unionists give it credit for; and we fancy it has a pretty shrewd idea as to how much the verdicts of free trippers are worth when they praise the hand that fed them.

FROM WARRINGTON TO PORTSMOUTH. A good deed never stops short at itself; it often grows by its force of example until the original is obscured by its off-shoots. The first summer camp for poor children led inevit- ably to many others being formed, and where a few poor Mites once benefited, thousands teoxnpecrile.iii:cpes, experience e joys of a visit to the seaside. MRS. BARRAULD. Anna Ltetitia Aikin,

Mrs. Barbauld,

- - Born 20th June, 1743. "We treasured each remembered name The stars in our eternal sky." This was the inscription put on a wreath placed on the memorial tablet at her former residence in Dial-street, to the memory of Mrs. Barbauld, on the anniversary of her birth. It is well that Alderman Bennett and his friends should keep alive the memory of this celebrated Warrington resident of the long ago. Mrs. Barbauld wrote the famous lines: Life! We've been long together, Through pleasant and through cloudy weather; 'Tis hard to part when friends are dear, Perhaps 'twill cost a sigh, a tear, Then steal away, give little warning; Choose thine own time; Say not " Good-night"; but in some brighter clime Bid me "Good-morning." Wordsworth said of this stanza: "I am not in the habit of grudging people their good things, but I wish I had written those lines."

===== 0002601_19140704_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A HAPPY AUGURY.

The progress already made in Warrington in connection with the forthcoming National Revival Campaign is very satisfactory, but a still greater cause for rejoicing is to be found in the fact that all religious denominations in the borough are acting in co-operation. For the first time in the history of the religious life of the town, Churchmen, Catholics, Noncon- formists, and other creeds were assembled on a common pkitform at the meeting held on Monday evening to further the scheme. Such co-operation is welcome, as it indicates that the sectarian barriers of the past are slowly , yet steadily being demolished.

OUR WILD FLOWER TABLE. A recent visit to the wild flower table at the Museum revealed the fact that this department of our municipal life is as flourishing as ever— and also that the neighbourhood of Warring- ton is passing rich just now in early summer flowers. The countryside seems to have com- pletely recovered from the adverse effects of a dry, cold May. Here were ranged no fewer than 35 grasses, forming a fine green and greenish border on two sides of the room for as fine and gay a collection of colour as could be well imagined. The dog rose, March cinquefoil, ragged robin, red campion, and the yellow iris, vigorous enough to suggest having been cultivated, made a pleasing contrast, while the cornfields supplied the spotted persicarid, the first of the poppies, corn marigold, corn spurrey, and corn chanomile. At one corner of the table nestled some growing wood sorrel, affirmed by many botanists to be the original shamrock of St. Patrick.

An alien which was imported by some means from North America—presumably with corn— and succeeded in establishing itself at Bixton Firs, 30 or 40 years ago, was worthily repre- sented by several sprigs of bright red attractive ; flowers. There are two clumps of this plant, the heathwort or lambkill, growing at Rixton, many yards long, and promising further pro- gress. Attempts made to transplant portions to other parts have signally failed, the plant apparently having a preference for peaty land and no liking at all for fertile soil. Sonic specimens of the hemlock water drop- wort are sßecially pointed out to youthful visitors for the dangers that lurk in its stems. This plant, which grows on the banks of 'rivers and streams and in wet places and has a hollow stem, is cut by children and made into a kind of whistle. But it could not be put to a worse use than this, for it is one of the most poisonous plants in the British flora. At the very least this kind of whistle will make the young musician's lips sore. When the plant is mistaken for wild celery, because of its peculiar smell, it is sometimes eaten with fatal results. One side of the room is still occupied by such out-of-the-way objects in Nature as water- spiders, stick insects (now in the sixth genera- tion), caddis worms and stoneloach. No won- der the children of the town find their way to the flower table in increasing numbers, or that many of the schools make its constant inspec- tion part of their organised system of study.

EVERYWHERE,

The empty head never has a light heart.

Hope is about the cheapest thing in the world.

Make people think better of themselves and you will not need to worry over your own epitaph.

A man thinks he has a natural capacity for history if he can remember the year his town won the football cup.

The Broken Chain.

"The Chains of Bondage" in two parts ran a recent local cinema advertisement. Surely the captive would be free?

The Brand She Liked.

_ He (in uniform) : "I love the smell of powder." She (delightedly) "Oh, do you? Don't you think the violent scent is the best?"

"Ile!"

;• Ile soled hear" is the notice to be seen in the window of a small Warrington shop. Yet the schoolmaster has been abroad for over 40 years.

Tire Wise Change Their Opinion. At the Warrington Borough Court it was said of a prisoner, charged with being drunk, that when he was being placed in the cell he stated, " This is the place for me—l am drunk." In the court he pleaded "not guilty."

Wasted. The tramp, wiping his mouth, emerged from a Warrington public-house to be enveloped in a dust cloud stirred up by a passing motor. "Blow me," he exclaimed, as he gained his breath, "there's another tuppence wasted."

The Tale of a Dog. A woman was bewailing the death of a favourite dog which had succumbed after swal- lowing a tape measure. "Poor dear," said her friend, "I suppose it died by inches." "No. it went round to the back of the house and died by the yard."

The Hot Weather. At the local Court on Tuesday a prisoner was stated to have been found asleep on the steps of the Museum. He was sent to a cool prison—evi- dently the simple life is not favoured in War- rington.

"All We Like Sheep Have Gone Astray." One in Buttermarket-street, on Monday night, however, did not think so. Politely but firmly it refused to "budge." The episode concluded by its two youthful keepers using it as a rope in a tug-of-war, to the amusement of the " unwashed " who gathered round. Every- body seemed excited except the offender

"THE OTHER IDOL." Suggested by a recent article in "The Examiner" headed "The Sun as Doctor." What though thy blessed beams above, On field and flowers glowing— Unearth for us a treasure-trove, With fragrant fruits o'erflowing?

What if the lark beneath thy rays With grateful song is soaring— It's cheery chant, in sweetest praise. Thy golden light adoring?

What though thy volume on the dews Spreads out, in coloured pages, "A promise writ in rainbow hues, And carried through the ages?"

Though countless germs, in sunless cells, With dread diseases teeming— Will perish all—our science tells— Where thy pure light is streaTning?

And though—thou idol of the days Ere faith revealed thy master— Men built an altar in thy rays To charm away disaster?

They found at length, though clouds may hide The splendour of thy dawning— Thy constancy would o'er abide " The spirit of the morning." Yet, constancy was lightly prized, Thy worth men failed to measure, And often since have idolised More marketable treasure. In vain thy cures and claims unfold Our mission here is "barter"! For now we worship other gold And man becomes its martyr. J. NORDVRI.

A LIBERAL STALWAR

Alderman William Pierpoint Honoured by the Party.

AN EXAMPLE FROM THE PAST.

On Wednesday night, in the large billiard room of the Liberal Club, Alderman William Pierpoint was presented with an enlarged framed photograph of himself, and what was properly called a handsome purse of gold, by his friends in the Liberal party, as a recogni- tion of the great services lie has rendered throughout a long life to the Progressive cause in Warrington. - The cotnmittee who had charge of the presen- tation and brought it to so successful an issue consisted of Alderman Tinnion, Councillors J. Wilkinson, J. T. Locker, S. Gough, Mr. William Hayes, and Mr. E. A. Sanders. Among those present were Alderman Pier- point, Alderman Tinnion, Councillors Wilkin- son, Locker, Gough, Lindley; Messrs. T. Locker, Jos. Wilkinson, W. Bibby, William Hayes, Thomas Critchley, W. 11. Dutton, James Sankey, T. O'Brien, Thos. Shaw, S. W. Jeffery, Robert Pierpoint (the Alderman's second son), William Pierpoint, William Patrick, Herbert Johnson (secretary of the Liberal Club), and E. A. Sanders (agent of the Liberal Association).
