A GLOWING TRIBUTE.

Worked for the Betterment of the

Conditions of the Poor.

TiAdlsdiesrmonaen fTtrNhoNiro friend, Mr. :moyneololotwsfiofLimberlalifes.. I have been associated with myyt proudest u si said: eto o what ' I started under his guidance ears. Alderman Pierpoint, for a great number of 3 t time I could for Liberalism when I came to War- rington as a youth. At that I found Mr. a Pierpoint, if not the most active, one of the th most active, members of e Liberal party, a t man who was bearing more than his share of he burden of carrying on the good work of Progressive Liberalism in the borough. He was then, and he has been ever since, not a Liberal of profession—he was a Liberal because he felt in his heart that the under-dog was worth working for, and that it was worth spend- ing his life for the betterment of the condi- tion of those who were not so happily placed as himself. (Applause.) I remember that at the many meetings we had, if there was a man on the platform who could arouse the sympathies and the feelings of any audience in Warrington, Mr. Pierpoint was that man. (Hear, hear.) Many a time he has turned an election, many a time he has turned a meeting, when things were not quite as favourable as they might have been. He was a man whose influence was felt not only among the rank and file of the party, but he had the confidence and admir- ation of the leaders of the party at that time. For many years he was the treasurer of the association. He was one of the men who found the money, saying, "I will get it back some day." It was only after the party had had the most successful bazaar ever held in Warring- ton that Mr. Pierpoint could say to them: "Well, you have cleared yourselves of debt to everybody, even including pae; now go on, fur- nished as you are with funds, and progress as you ought to do. I have done what I could to help you, and you have now relieved me of my responsibility. Everything is bright and happy before you. Go on 'and prosper." (Ap- plause.) He was not only treasurer, but chair- man of the Liberal Association for a long time, a thorough fighter, a hard worker, who believed in fulfilling every duty belonging to any office he held. (Hear, hear.) At that time the Chairman of the Executive was very largely responsible for the fighting attitude and ele- ment of the party, and faithfully and well did he do his duty as Chairman of the Executive. (Hear, hear.) He was intimately connected with this club, and it was through his instru- mentality and that of your old friend, and our old friend, Mr. Fleming—(applause)—that this club passed through its difficulties and was saved at that time, much to the satisfaction, I feel sure, of every one of you present. (Hear, hear.) If any man should have credit, apart from Mr. McMinnies and Mr. John Grosfield, that credit is due to Mr. William Pierpoint. Since lie put the association and the club on a sound and satisfactory basis the club has never looked back; it is now, and long has been, one of the healthiest institutions in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) It has become the only real and permanent home of Liberalism in Warrington. It shelters under its roof every part of the organisation of Liberalism in Warrington. It has found a home for the parent association, and for another very good and useful auxiliary, the Women's Liberal Association. (Applause.) It has also found a home for the Labour League —(hear, hear)—and I think it has found a home for all these bodies of which I think we may be proud. I have been a member of this club long enough to see around me many wen whom I know to be very old members, and I have often heard them speak of the happy times they have spent within its walls. (Applause.)

Scarlet Fever Epidemic. There were 92 cases of scarlet fever in the Warrington Hospital last week. Nineteen new cases had been reported.

Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank. Mr. Henry Whitehead, M.V.0., has been unanimously elected deputy-chairman of this bank in place of the late Colonel 0. Ormerod Walker.

Passed With llonours. Mr. Arthur Lee, of Warrington, has passed his intermediate examination with honours at the Society of Incorporated Accountants' and Auditors' Examination, held last month.

Births and Deaths. The births in Warrington last week num- bered 43, giving a rate of 31.2, compared with 39.3 in the same week last year. The deaths totalled 25, giving a rate of 17.3, compared with 11• 9 last year. 1

Territorial Notes. A well-attended battalion parade was held in the Drill Hall on Tuesday night.—The annual prize shoot takes place to-day at Apple- ton.—Free gymnastic classes for battalion mem- bers are now held at the Drill Hall on Wednes- days, conducted by Mr. M. Naval!.

Motor Cars Collide. Whilst being driven along Froghall-lane on Saturday morning by the chauffeur, Councillor Roberts' motor car, containing Mrs. Roberts, collided with Dr. Elodgkinson's car, which was coming out of a side-street. Although both cars were damaged, everybody, fortunately, escaped injury.

Fatal Fall Downstairs. Mr. S. Brighouse conducted an inquest at Warrington on Wednesday on Frank Wood (27), carter, lodging at 6, Howley-lane.—lt was stated that deceased fell downstairs at his lodgings. He was removed to the Workhouse Hospital, where he died.—" Accidental death" was the verdict returned.

Three Warrington M.D.'s. The following have had conferred upon them the degree of M.D. of the Liverpool University: J. Bamforth, son of Mr. Bamforth, chemist, Winwick-street; R. Kennon, son of Mr. Kennon, Manchester- road; H. Nield, son of the late Councillor Nield.

Local Government Board Inquiry. Mr. Edgar Dudley, one of the Local Govern- ment Board's inspectors, will visit the Town Hall on Friday next to hold an inquiry into the Corporation's application to borrow £1,410 for property purchasing and the redemption of ground rents in connection with the widening of Cockliedge-lane, linutsford-road and Oliver- street.

A Shunter's Fatal Fall. After 35 years' service with the London and North-Western Railway Company, William Naylor, foreman shunter, Pairclough-street, Earlestown, was run over and killed by a shunted waggon at Bank Quay Station on Mon- day during shunting operations. At the in- quest on Wednesday a verdict of " Accidental death" was returned, it being shown that he accidentally fell in frodt of the waggon.

Royal Ruby Cycles. The enormous growth of the Royal Ruby business has necessitated the acquirement of greatly extended premises, and the works of the company at Cannal-street, Ancoats, Man- chester, are now being more than doubled in size. It is hoped with these extensions to cope with the very rapidly growing demand for Royal Ruby cycles, and motor cycles, due to the wonderful value the company is giving to its customers.

Local Prize Winners at Royal Show. The 75th annual show of the Royal Agricul- tural Society opened at Shrewsbury on Tues- day. Many prizes were won by the King. Several .animals died from the great heat.

Sir Gilbert Greenell's exhibits were winners in the pig classes. For Shire mares, with foal at foot, Mr. S. S. Raingill, Altrincham, was awarded second prize. Mr. J. Makeague, New- ton-le-Willows, was second in the hackney filly class (foaled in 1912).

Royal Court Theatre Picnic. The Royal Court Theatre staff held their annual picnic on Sunday, when 43 members left for Lancaster by an early train. They were conveyed in three char-a-bancs to the village of Beetham, near Milnthorpe, where they had dinner at Mr. Dobson's. After an enjoyable afternoon, spent in wandering through the woods, tea was partaken of, the return being made through Arnside, Silverdale, and Carnforth, where a mail train to Warring- ton was caught. The arrangements was ably supervised by Mr. H. L. Warburton, the acting- manager of the theatre.

Museums' Association. The 25th annual meeting of the Museums' Association will be held at Swansea from the 6th to 10th July, under the presidency of Mr. Charles 3fadeley, director of the Warrington Municipal Museum, who will deliver his pre- sidential address upon the second day. This will be followed by a series of papers, including "The Accepted Design for the Belfast Art Gallery and Ikluseum," by Mr. Arthur Deane, formerly of the local Museum. Other War- rington representatives will be Alderman A. Bennett and Mr. It Stuart Page; whilst Mr. Thomas May, of Crieff, until recently a member of the Warrington Museum Committee, will pis° be a delegate.

Local Government Officers Confer. The National Association of Local Govern- ment Officers (North-Western District) held a meeting at the Town Hall on Saturday after- noon. About 30 delegates from Warrington, Barrow, Southport, , Lancaster, Rochdale, Lytham, Chester, Bolton, Prestwich, Liverpool, Manchester, and Salford attended, and the War- rington representatives were Messrs. Charles Madeley, J. M. Murray, T. S. Mather, 31.. A. Piercy, and J. McAdam (local hon. secretary). Mr. Duthrie (director of education for Salford) presided. The agenda of the annual meeting to be held to-day at Ilkley was considered. It was decided to increase the subscriptions from local associations from Is. per head per year to 2s. Tea was provided by the local asso- ciation. The Mayor telegraphed his regret at being unable to be present, but wishing the gathering success.

Travellers' Secretary Honoured. The quarterly general meeting of the War- rington branch of the United Kingdom Com- mercial Travellers' Association was held at the Lion Hotel on Saturday evening. The Chair- man (Mr. P. O'Brien) presided over a good attendance of members, which included Mr. T. Percival (hon. secretary) and Mr. W. L. Walker (hon. assistant secretary). Mr. O'Brien and Mr. W. G. Hayes gave a very interesting report of the annual conference of the association, which was held in Birmingham during Whit Week. They both spoke of the good work being done by the association, both from a business point of view and also in the cause of charity. Mr. O'Brien said he was much impressed with the fact that such a body of men, gathered as they were from all parts of the kingdom, were quite prepared and willing to help their brother mem- bers in any trouble they might have. Mr. Hayes spoke of the various motions which had been discussed at the conference, some of which will no doubt prove beneficial to commercial travel- lers generally. A vote of thanks' proposed by Mr. 11. Mercer, to the delegates for attending the conference and for their report of same, was heartily carried. The Chairman said he had a very pleasing duty to perform on behalf of the members, and that was to offer their congratulations to the Hon. Secretary on the occasion of his marriage. He spoke of the able manner in which the secre- tarial work of the branch was carried on, and, as a token of the esteem in which he was held, presented a handsome silver tea and coffee ser- vice and a silver jewel casket as a special present for Mrs. Percival. Mr. Percival, in thanking the members, said he had been secre- tary for only a short time, but was pleased to think that he had made so many friends.

Captain Waring. Captain Waring, who is staying at Llanfair- fechan with Mrs. and •Miss Waring, has im- proved much by the change.

Progressive Labour League. Mr. A. H. Ainley, of Salford, will he the speaker at the meeting at Bridge Foot on Monday.

Church Lads in Camp. The St. Paul's Company of the Church Lads' Brigade yesterday commenced their march to Sutton Weaver, where they will spend the week-end under canvas.

Office and Household Furniture Sale.

A portion of the office furniture at the Taylor Mill's Mutual Self-Help Money Society's Office, as well as a quantity of household furniture and effects will be sold at 66, Buttermarket- street by Mr. Henry Taylor On Thursday.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 4, 1914.

Rain.

Children' s Annual Festival Marred by Pitiless

===== 0002601_19140704_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
YET SOME MAKE A BRAVE SHOW.

" Pitter-pat, pitter-pat," steadily fell the rain on the ground as the children assembled yester- day in Bank Park to form into the annual Walking Day procession. The conditions were of a character calculated to depress, the stoutest heart. The elder processionists felt it so, and the suits and frocks which they had anticipated wearing with marked effect upon this the great day of the year were promptly hidden under mackintoshes. The elder children carried, like the seniors, umbrellas and waterpiioofs, but even the falling of the merciless rain was not sufficient to damp the ardour of the youngest children, and they went through the proceedings ' and rain with glee and pleasure. The downpour decidedly marred our great annual function, and although the Church of England section, to which was attached a num- ber of non-sectarian schools, carried through their part of the street programme, it was executed amidst most trying conditions. Spectators lined the streets wase n S troute,ilnl thousands ad nmgave th " royal" welcome. To cope with such a crowd . but once more the War- rington police were capable of performing the task. Chief Constable Martin Nicholls had a

WHERE THE AFTERNOON WAS

SPENT.

The processionists afterwards adjourned to fields in the vicinity of Warrington, where sports and games were held. The schools and places were:— Blue Coat—School grounds. Fairfield—Training College field. ,National—Training College field. St. James'—Wilderspool. St. George's—Training College field. All Saints'—Bewsey Park. St. Paul's—Bewsey Park. St. Ann's—Barracks Recreation Ground. St. Barnabas'—Farnworth. St. Clement's—St. Elphin's Park. Ragged School—Grappenhall Reyes. CATHOLIC PROCESSIONS TO-MORROW. The rainstorm_ was at its height yesterday when the Catholic procession should have started, and it was abandoned. Last night it was announced that each Catholic Church would hold its procession separately on Sunday. .

===== 0002601_19140704_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REV. T. T. JAMES AND HIS WYCLIFFE FLOCK About to start in the rain for Hartford.

full force of 80 men out, and, along with Chief Superintendent Williamson as second in com- mand, they good-humouredly kept the crowds of spectators well in hand.

AN UMBRELLA PARADE. Jupiter Pluvius was emptying his water butts with a vengeance as the procession began to move out of the Park. Gathered on the steps of the Town Hall were a number of Aldermen and Councillors, and their friends, whilst in the midst of this umbrella sheltered group were the Mayor and Mayoress, who took the salutes of the youngsters as they filed past. Five minutes before‘ the hour fixed, Chief Marshal R. F. Fairclough, and his assistant, Mr. Lionel Fairclough, out of regard for the children under such miserable conditions, set the procession on its way. The majority of the processionists carried umbrellas, which prompted a wag in the crowd to christen the function "An Umbrella Pro- cession," and so it will-be recorded in history, for it is doubtful if the Walking Day has ever before experienced worse weather, although it has had rain in previous years.

OLDEST SUNDAY SCHOOL.

A pleasing feature was the display made by scholars of St. James', which was established in 1779, and thus is said to be the oldest of its kind in England. The Territorial Band set the pace for a large percentage of the 800 children who attend the school to receive instruction from some 32 teachers. The youngsters, bright and neat despite the rain, were under the com- mand of Mr. W. H. Stansfield, and the clergy- men in attendance were the Revs. 11. B. Firth and H. H. Corsbie. In the matter of banners, St. James' School was particularly well represented. There were quite a number of them, all pretty and well decorated. All were representative of ecclesi- astical design. Chief amongst them was a large banner, overshadowing the rest, emblematical of St. James.

ST. ANN'S. A bright group of children, numbering nearly 400, was the quota sent to swell the ranks of the processionists from St. Ann's. The Rev. J. Davies led the van, and the music en route was purveyed by Walker's Band. Some 20 teachers looked after the welfare of the youngsters, under the superintendence of Miss Bradshaw, Mr. A. Bradshaw, and Mr. W. Thomas. There were some pretty banners and bannerettes, the large banner proving most imposing.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME FEATURES.

The honour of leading the van fell to the Bluecoat School, and they seemed to appreciate that honour. Then came school after school in quick succession. Each had its characteris- tic features, and the whole combined to make a pretty spectacle, a sight which even defied the persistent falling rain. Had the weather been such as was experienced earlier in the week the spectacle would have been a brilliant one. There was little novelty in the characters, and what there was was spoilt by the weather con- ditions. All Saints' Mission came along with, perhaps, the most striking tableau. It repre- sented a wedding group. A little lad, in frock coat and silk hat, played the part of bridegroom and an equally small tot of a girl, dressed in white, with veil and orange blossom, sedately represented the bride. She had a following of bridesmaids and train-bearers.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
MORRIS DANCERS.

Buckley-street Mission perhaps had the most , elaborate display of all. They were led by a bevy of pretty young girl Morris dancers, who danced all the way. Then came other lasses representing lilies, fairies, &c. and, towering above all, was a shepherd with his crook. The Ragged School wound up the procession, which took 55 minutes to pass through the Park Gates. Their most striking character was that of Britannia, but she who is supposed to rule the waves seemed out of her element in the rain. It was computed that close upon 10,000 children and adults took part in the procession. The principal details connected with each school are as follows :

BLUE COAT SCHOOL LADS. To a tuneful march by their own band, some seventy lads and lasses of the Blue Coat School, under the command of the master, Mr. E. Rose, and teachers, swung along with a martial stride. They were the successors of those who entered the school on its foundation in 1677, when such a procession was undreamt of, and very smart did they look in their characteristic uniforms. The chief banner of the school repre- sented the Bible, crown and sceptre, and there were a number of bannerettes.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE HOMES.

-- Following the leaders of the procession—the Blue Coat School—came the children and teachers from the Padgate Homes. Even the heavy rain was not sufficient to damp the ardour of the boys and girls from the Homes, who were evidently out to enjoy themselves. They were under the charge of the Superintendent of the Homes, Mr. Lawrence, and were headed by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell and Mrs. Hall, representing the Board of Guardians.

FAIRFIELD AND NATIONAL SCHOOLS'. Very pretty were the youngsters from the Fairfield and National Schools, and they were led by the famous prize band known throughout the musical world as " Crosfield's." The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) led this section of the.pro- cession, and some 500 daintily-dressed children, under the charge of Miss E. White (girls), Miss E. Hopkins (infants), and upwards of 20 teachers. A number of members of the Bible Class, &c., also attended, and the Church Lads' Brigade were also in evidence. . The National School was headed by the Rev. S. G. Stanton. The boys, who looked smart, were commanded by Mr. G. W. Brown, and the infants were under the careful charge of Miss A. Brown. Some nicely designed dresses were worn by the younger children, who braved the elements most cheerfully.

ST. GEORGE'S SCHOOL. St. George's School made a fine array, when a goodly number of the 710 children on the roll took part. They marched along to the tuneful strains of the Grappenhall Prize Band, and were headed by the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster. About 36 teachers accompanied the children, in

===== 0002601_19140704_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. PAUL'S AND ALL SAINTS'.

- - - A brave show was made by the St. Paul's scholars. The Ardwick Green Industrial School Band led about 500 of the children with 35 teachers along the route, and the youngsters entered enthusiastically into the fun of the day. They were led by their genial Vicar, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, whose right-hand assistants were Mrs. Bracecamp and Mr. A. J. Jolley. The children from All Saints' Mission, who followed immediately behind were headed by the Bugle Band and about 70 members of the St. Paul's C.L.8., under the command of Capt. Henshall and Lieut. Bottomley. The scholars numbered about 600, and amongst the pleasing characters were two tiny tots bedecked in bridal array, who looked exceedingly happy on their "wedding day." It was a pity the only confetti was the rain. The Rev. E. Downham and Messrs. Parks and Lionel Baker, along with some 40 other teachers were in charge of the scholars from this mission.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
HOLY TRINITY.

The scholars and teachers connected with the Trinity, Factory Yard Mission and St. Luke's Sunday Schools showed that the inclement weather could not mar a good turnout, for there were many pleasing characters, headed by the Walton Band. The Vicar, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, and Mrs. Carleton were imongst those who walked at the head of these schools.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. BARNABAS'.

As became such an important school, they made a worthy display, and their contribution to the procession was much appreciated. The Rev. J. 11. G. Hollingworth led a large number of his 680 children, and the management of them was in the careful hands of Mrs. J. H. G. Hollingworth and Mr. W. P. James, who were ably assisted by 28 teachers. The Preston Brook Brass Band dispensed suitable music, and the chief ornamentation was the banner of St. Barnabas.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
BUCKLEY-STREET.

The contribution by Buckley-street was, as usual, very pretty. To the strains of the youth- ful band of the Salford Union, little fairies, lilies and Morris dancers stepped out bravely along the wet streets, and won more than ordinary praise. Milkmaids, shepherdesses, Japanese ladies and other characters made themselves great favourites, and a banner held aloft, bearing the words, "Here we are again. Get your coppers ready," did not refer to the policemen, but to the good work they do in making a collection on behalf of the Infirmary. One of the prettiest items in the whole of the procession was the fairy queen attended by a host of fairy subjects.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
SALVATION ARMY.

The Salvation Army mustered about 120 children under the care of Adjutant Joseph Beaumont, Sergt.-Major J. Siddle, Messrs. W. H. Jones, secretary; J. Lee. bandmaster; S. Morrison, W. Urmstone, G. Ayres. and 18 teachers. Two bands were provided, the Army silver band and the boys' band. A feature of this group in the procession was a perambulator richly decorated with roses.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RAGGED SCHOOL.

The children of the Ragged School made a goodly show, mustering nearly 40, under the charge of Councillor Skelland and a large com- pany of teachers. The school band was respon- sible for the march music, which they played particularly well. There were some pretty tableaux, including those of " Rule, Britannia," and the fairies.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NONCONFORMISTS.

Most Spend Day in the Country.

As is customary, the Nonconformist schools did not take part in the procession, but started in the morning for the country, where they held their annual outing. It was computed that the children and friends who left Warrington yesterday on such picnics were not far short of 10,000. Unfortunately, the weather, as unfavourable at the start, but it improved later, and most were able to carry through the greater part of their programmes. The schools and their arrangements were as follow:

LEGH-STREET PRIMITIVE METHODIST. About 180 scholars and 28 teachers, under the charge of the Rev. A. J. Chessum, assembled at the Schoolroom, erected in 1873, marching to Bank Quay immediately, where a train was caught to Prodsham. Here they proceeded to Mersey Mount, where dinner and tea were provided. Valuable services were rendered in the games and sports by Messrs. J. Bissell, hon. superintendent; B. T. Parkes, superintendent; J. Himsworth, assistant superintendent; A. Cresswell, secretary; A. Sankey, assistant secre- tary; and A. E. Hoyle, president of Brother- hood. Many parents and friends accompanied the scholars, and an enjoyable day was spent.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
CAIRO-STREET UNITARIAN.

The school was established in 1768, and now has 220 scholars and 14 teachers, who did not take part in the procession, but took an early train to Lymm, under the guidance of Messrs. D. Plinston and F. Hollingworth. The field at Lymm, in which so enjoyable a time was passed, had been kindly lent by Mr. John Houghton, of West Leigh, Lymm.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN.

Mr. Taylor's field at Barrow Hall proved an ideal retreat for the 300 scholars and 25 teachers of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan School, under the Rev. H. Middlewood (Warrington Circuit), Messrs. James Gorst and Walter Wilkes, super- intendents; A. Corradine, secretary; and C. Johnson, treasurer. A well-arranged proces- sion, with 'The Sower" banner, met at the school ana marched along the main road to the field, at which dinner and teft were provided for the children, and a grand sports programme carried out, a prize being given for each class.

BRICK-STREET SCHOOL. Headed by their own band, and in the care of Mr. James Shaw, superintendent, and Mr. W. Yates Dutton, 400 scholars and 24 teachers marched from the school early in the morning, with "The Guardian Angel" banner, to their field at Padgate, via Lythgoes-lane, Ilaydoek- street, Horsemarket-street, Buttermarket-street, Manchester-road, and Padgate-lane. Excellent dinners and teas were served at the field, and in the afternoon the annual cricket match— Married v. Single—took place, providing much interest. In the evening the band gave many selections before the departure for home.

ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN. In accordance with their usual custom, about 100 scholars and nine teachers of the school, established as English Presbyterian in 1854, under the care of the Rev. William Reid and Mr. Robert Mimgall (secretary), left Bank Quay by special train for Vrodsham, returning at eight o'clock in the evening.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH WESLEYAN.

. _...__... _ About 220 scholars and 23 teachers, under the Rev. H. Middlewood, Messrs. Withinshaw and ' Ambler, superintendents; and Wilfred Leigh, secretary, assembled at the school early in the afternoon, and, accompanied by the Pioneer Band. Warrington, proceeded along the War- rington, Heath and Greystone roads, through the village and round to the school for tea. In the evening the usual sports were held on the Recreation Ground, after which the scholars again assembled in the schoolroom for the pre- sentation of the prizes.

GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST. Leaving for Acton Bridge by a morning train, 2qo scholars and 14 teachers of the Baptist School (established 1869), in the charge of the , Rev. H. Ogden, Messrs. R. Buckby, superin- ' tendent; T. Longshaw, secretary; and H. Jones, assistant secretary, assembled upon arrival and joined in singing the anniversary hymns, ac- companied by the Free Church Boys' Brigade I(Golborne-street Company) Band, after which ~ adjourned to the field kindly lent by Mrs. Rigby. Dinner and tea were served in the Acton Bridge Sunday School. Iloop-las and Aunt Sallies were well patronised, whilst games, races and competitions were held. Young and old returned at dusk, deliciously tired.

WORKING MEN'S MISSION. The e,cholars from the school connected with the Working Men's Mission marched in Proce9-

millilMll

sion to Bank Quay Station, headed by the Boys' Brigade and their band. The party proceeded to Daresbury, where, through the kindness cf Mr. Gibbons, a field was put at their disposal. Games and sports wiled away the hours. Both_ dinner and tea were provided in a. large tent. Messrs. A. Tilling, P. Gleave and A. Walker were responsible for the sports. The party numbered about 1,000, for whom the arrange- ments had been made by Messr.a. N. Hough (president), G. Dale (vice-president), T. Dale and Win. Worrall (superintendents), R. Tanner (treasurer), and A. Edwards (secretary).

SILVER-STREET WESLEYANIS. The scholars of this Sunday school, led by the Warrington Pioneer Band, arched t.) Bank Quay Station early in the morning, prior to their departure for Helsby, where fr. Albert Wright, of School Farm, had kindly put a field at their disposal, in which games And sports were indulged in. Amongst those who had made the arrangements Were the Rei: W. A. ===== 0002601_19140704_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
lintitsford Liberals' Fete.

The Knutsford Division Liberals hold a fete and sports at Kingsley, Hill Cliff e, next Satur- day. The speakers will be Lord Sheffield, Mr. John Wad, M.P., and Mr. P. M. Oliver.

New Co-operative Premises. Next Saturday Mr. J. Upson, president of the Warrington Co-operative Society, will perform the opening ceremony at the new premises in Willis-street. Prior to that there will be a procession of decorated vehicles.

Sale by Auction. Mr. Frank H. Fellows will conduct a sale by auction of useful household furniture and effects and the fixtures and fittings at "Golden Rule," 87, Buttermarket-street, and 24, Mersey- street, on Tuesday. For further particulars see advertisement.

Warrington Gas Profits. In the annual report of the Warrington gas department it is stated that the profits are £5,829 less than the previous year, due to in- creased cost of coal, lower prices for residuals and increased cost of repairs. Wages were also more. The net profit was ...t8,619 out of a gross profit of £16,620.

'Bus Driver's Promptitude. The promptness and coolness with which a Market Gate and Gorsey-lane 'bus driver handled his 'bus was responsible for an acci- dent being narrowly averted at noon yesterday. With the intention of joining his companion on the other side of Orford-avenue, a little boy ran in front of the approaching 'bus and only escaped being run over by a few inches. The driver is to be complimented on his action in bringing the vehicle to a standstill in so short a space.

Death of Mr. Joseph Twiss. It is with deep regret that we record the death of Mr. Joseph Twiss, which took place at his residence, 27, Stafford-road, on Thursday. Mr. Twiss, who was 63 years of age, had been in failing health since Easter. He was a naan- aging director of the Warrington Estates Co., and leaves a widow, one son (who went to New Brunswick. Canada, 12 months ago), and two daughters. The funeral will take place at Hill Cliffe on Monday at 2.30.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Youth Drowned.

A well-known Stockton Heath youth named William Pugh (18), son of Mr. and Mrs. Peter Pugh, 10, Leonard-street, was -drowned in the Bridgewater Canal, at Preston Brook, on Thurs- day night, the body being recovered early yes- terday morning. Up to six weeks ago he worked at the Stockton Heath Forge, but went on one of the canal flats to improve his health. The inquest will be held this morning at Dutton Workhouse.

Children's Summer Camp. The committee of the Warrington Children's Summer Camp at Prestatyn have arranged a picnic for Thursday, 23rd July, and this func- tion, which affords visitors every opportunity of seeing the good work done in the camp, should be largely patronised. The train leaves Bank Quay at 10.23 a.m. and returns from Pres- tatyn at 6.27. The ticket, which includes fare, lunch and tea in the camp, costs only Ss. 6d. Those who desire to go on the trip should notify Mr. E. E. Bradshaw, acting hon. secretary, 4, Egypt-street, at once.

Children at Cinema Shows. The Watch Committee have considered the recommendations of the Education Committee about children attending cinema shows, and re- solved that children apparently under 14 years should not be admitted to such places after 9 p.m. unless accompanied by an adult; and that in the case of an epidemic of infectious disease the committee may direct the exclusion of chil- dren under 14. The proposal that children shoukl be excluded during school hours was not approved. The Health Committee have consid- ered the same matter and have arrived at a similar decision.

Preachers for To-morrow. Wycliffe Congregational—Morning and even- ing, Rev. T. T. James.

'daristadelphians—Evening, Mr. W. Stewart, Droylsden. _ _ _

.. Bold-street Wesleyan—Morning, Rev. H. Wil- liamson; evening, Rev. W. A. Beckett.

Bewsey-road *esleyan—Morning, Rev. W. A. Beckett; evening, Rev. H. H. Wade. _ _ .

Latchford Baptist—Morning and evening, Rev. J. S. Chedburn.

St. John's Presbyterian—Morning and even- ing, Rev. William Reid. Golborne-street Baptist—Morning and even- ing, Rev. H. Ogden. Cairo-street—Morning and evening, Rev. P. Moore.

Parish Church Ohl Choristers' Association.

The presentation of book prizes provided by the above association to the choirboys of the Parish Church took place on Wednesday even- ing. The President (Mr. F. J. Dell) presided, and was supported by the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis), the 'Rev. J. M. Buckmaster, Messrs. F. M. Darby, Thomas Mason, R. T. Harding, A. Simkin, and Walter Dennett.—Mr. Darby spoke highly of the conduct of the boys, and Mr. Dell, in a neat speech, exhorted all the boys to stick to the church.—A vote of thanks was accorded the President, on the proposition of the Rector, seconded by Mr. Thomas Mason.

Church Brigade Lad's Funeral. A most impressive funeral service was con- ducted on Saturday afternoon in the Warring- ton Cemetery, when over a hundred members of the local Battalion of the Church Lads' Brigade attended to pay respect to one of their late comrades. The deceased, William Ellison, the 19-year-old son of Mr. and Mrs. J. Ellison, 24, Cartwright-street, was a private in the St. Paul's Company, and was a most enthusiastic member of the cyclist section, the members of whom were the bearers. The first part of the service was held in St. Paul's Church, where the Company Chaplain (the Bev. W. Brace- camp) officiated. The mournful procession wended its way to the Cemetery, and after the last rites had been recited by the Rev. E. Down- ham, three volleys were fired and the, buglers sounded the "Last Post" over the grave.

Teachers' Picnic and Presentation. Unusual interest attended the annual picnic of the Warrington and District Teachers' Asso- ciation, held at the Pleasure Grounds, Thelwall Brook, on Saturday, when bowling mains were held by the ladies and gentlemen in the after- noon and evening respectively. A handsome music cabinet was presented after tea to the Assistant Director of Education (Mr. A. S. Elliot), on the occasion of his recent marriage. In presenting the cabinet, Mr. George Newburn, president, said that one of the most pleasing features of the presentation was the hearty response which the committee had received from practically every teacher in the town, showing the high esteem in which Mr. Elliot was held, and the good feeling which existed between the officials of the Education Authority and the teaching profession.—Mr. Elliot, who was loudly applauded, thanked the teachers for the graciousness which had prompted them to make the presentation. He hoped the present good feeling would be continued, as they had all one end in view—the welfare and education of the children.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYANS.

Complete preparations had been macii, for the scholars of Bold street Wesleyan Sunday school to spend a most enjoyable day at Acton Bridge, where, in a field belonging to Mr. George MOSS, of Milton' Farm, sports were held. Over 300 teachers, officers and children made the journey from Bank Quay Station. The Bev. Hugh Wil- liamson, together with Messrs. Wm. Silcock, F. Light and R. Ostle, were in charge of the arrangements.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL.

Assembling early at the school, about 300 scholars and friends marched through the prin- cipal streets, accompanied by a band, to Bank Quay Station, where they entrained for the pretty village of Hartford, on the borders of Delamere Forest. The arrangements were in the hands of the Rev. T. T. James, Messrs. Win. Brown (superintendent), Herbert Eccles (secre- tary), and Gordon E. Pickton (ngsktant secre- tary). Races and games were held at the field, and a splendid day was concluded with several selections from the band.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH WESLEYANS.

Over 200 scholars and teachers from the Stock- ton Heath Wesleyan Sunday school assembled in the schoolroom early in the morning, and boarding special tramcars they proceeded to Bank Quay Station in readiness for their visit to Frodsham, where the Mersey View provided the chief attraction. Dinner and tea were served by the teachers and workers, and for the latter meal a good number of parents and friends were present. The party was in charge of the superintendents, Messrs. T. Deakin and J. Asher, and T. Brereton and G. Whitfield (secretaries).

===== 0002601_19140704_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
BANK DIVIDENDS.

Lancashire and Yorkshire.

The dire-a-OW-Of -tile-Lancashire and York- shire Bank, Ltd., yesterday declared an interim dividend for the past half-year at the rate of seventeen shillings per share, subject to deduc- tion of Income Tax. The dividend last year was at the same rate.

London City and :Midland. The directors of the London City and Mid- land Bank, Limited, announce an interim divi- dend for the past half-year at the rate of 18 per cent. per annum, free of income tax, payable on the 15th inst. The dividend for the corre- sponding period last year was at the same rate.

Manchester and Liverpool District. The Manchester bank dividends announced yesterday indicate little change in the position as compared with the first half of last year. The Manchester and Liverpool District is paying 171 per cent, per annum, free of tax, for the twenty-third consecutive half-year, adding £lO,OOO to the reserve and a like sum to the pension fund, as it did last year, and carrying forward £52,121, as against £47,331 a year ago and .£49,465 at the close of 1913, when the re- serve fund was made up to £1,600,000.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON IDOL? SCHOOLS.

At the Sankey Bridges Men's on Sunday Mr. John Monks gave a lecturette on "Is religion. helpful?" The speaker contended that whether anyone denied the existence of God or not, they believed in "something." That "something" was to them their god. Duman society could not exist in communities without a religious idea as the foundation of that existence. Deal- ing with the mental aspect of religion, Mr. Monks said life was a complex thing; they could not account for it or-understand it from a materialistic outlook. Materialism took them so far and then failed them just at the crucial , moment. Beyond the material, however, was the spiritual. After a lengthy discussion on some of the finer points raised by Mr. Monks, he was thanked for his very suggestive address. The Bible lesson for the day, "Nahum, the hatred of the oppressor," was introduced by Mr. David Ridyard, who said it was satisfactory to see that real knowledge of the Old Testament was increasing.

STOCKTON HEATH MEN'S. On Sunday morning Mr. J. King delivered a lecturette on "Clay, glass and metal." After briefly outlining his subjects, the speaker ex- plained their uses by mixing them together in order to obtain various results. In order to explain his subject he brought a good display of articles, both in the rough and fin- ished states. The lecture was very instructive and keenly followed. Many questions were asked and answered very satisfactorily. An in- spection of the various specimens followed. Mr. W. H. Sherburn introduced the Bible lesson on "Nahum."

Death of Mr. W. 11. Mayers. Yesterday afternoon the death occurred at his residence, 92. Priestley-street, of Mr. Wil- liam Henry Mayers, after a severe illness of six weeks. The deceased, whose age was 49, was an off-licence holder, and for many years had held the office of president of the Off-Licence Associa- ' tion. For 26 years he had been a member of the " Admiral Nelson" Lodge of the Oddfellows, and was also connected with the Order of Buffaloes and the Warrington Conservative Club. Mr. Mayers leaves a widow and two daughters to mourn their loss.

WARRINGTON RURAL COUNCIL.

Sankey Water and Sankey Brook

Once More.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bewsey Chambers, on Wednesday, Councillor W. Furness in the chair. The CLERK (Mr. T. C. Sutton) said he was in receipt of a letter from the Local Govern- ment Board relating to children's holidays. He was told that there were occasions when such children were received at parts of the district, and he had given a copy of the Local Govern- ment Board's letter to the health officers. The INSPECTOR (Mr. F. Kerfoot) said they had not been lately; he believed the last were at Burtonwood. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL: We are glad they don't now come; they are regular pests. The INSPECTOR: I have not heard of any for some time. Mr. MITCHELL: I believe they had to be stopped. Councillor DENNETT jocularly drew attention to Mr. Mitchell's allusion to the children as pests. Mr. Mrrcimr,: It is not the children who are pests, it is their training. We are peaceful citizens at Brirtonwood and we want to live in peace. (Laughter.)

San key Water.

- On the invitation of the Chairman, the In- spector gave the offer of Mr. James Gray, the Warrington Corporation water manager, who, in a letter, said he was prepared to recommend the Water Committee to make an extension of the water main along Hood-lane, Sankey, from the present termination to opposite cottages on the south side of the railway and near the railway bridge, provided that the Rural District Council enter into an agreement of ..t2:2 per annum for a term of 20 years. It was stated the money would come from the Sankey Parish Council. The CHAIRMAN said every source of supply had been exhausted. Several wells had dried up at the top of Hood-lane, and he was afraid -that the conditions were very unpleasant: As a District Council, it Was their duty to do something. He fully anticipated that the Sankey Parish Coun- cil would not be called to pay anything. The matter was referred to the Palish Council.

Obstruction at Sankey Brook. The SURVEYOR stated that he- Called on the Town Clerk of Warrington the previous day, and was told that the Town Clerk had written a firm letter to Mr. Selby (Lord Lilford's agent) re the obstruction at Sankey Brook. The Sur- veyor added that he had examined the brook and found the obstructions worse than ever. He suggested writing a similar letter on behalf of the Council, and failing any improvement the Borough and District Councils should undertake the. work and recover the cost from Lord Lilford. This was agreed to.

Literary Gleanings.

THE PRINCE AND THE WAITER.

In "Sly Varied Life," Mr. F. C. Phillips, soldier, barrister, novelist, journalist, and play- wright, tells some capital stories. One of them arose out of trouble the Savage Club had with the licensing authorities: " The Prince of Wales and the Grand Duke Alexis went into the smok- ing-room, which was empty, and the Prince, after consultation with the Grand Duke, ordered two whiskies and soda. 'I have to ask your name, sir,' said the waiter in attendance. 'I do not think I have seen you before.' 'My name is Albert Edward,' said the Prince. The waiter immediately referred to the list of mem- bers. 'I see an Edwards here,' he said, 'but his name is not Albert. Ido not see your name here, sir.' 'Well,' said the Prince, 'I am a member, and my name is Albert Edward.' 'Well, sir,' said the waiter, who, after the late bother, was terrified out of his life, 'I am afraid I cannot serve you; I cannot find your name and it is more than my place is worth to do it.' Happily, at that moment a member en- tered the smoking-room, who, like most other people, knew the Prince by sight and everything was, of course, put right, the unfortunate waiter nearly dying of fright." Some of the tales concern the Bar. Mr. Baron Martin at the Guildford Assizes, trying a man for murder, summed up against the pris- oner. The jury, taking a merciful view of the case, returned a verdict of manslaughter. Baron Martin, always brief in his sentences, never attempted to harrow a prisoner's feelings. " Preesoner at the bar," said the old Scot, "you're the luckiest man I ever came across. Tak' penal servitude for life."

===== 0002601_19140704_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MELANESIANS.

Captain W. Sinker, writing in "East and West," gives some details about the people on the Melanesian Islands, among whom he worked as commander of the mission steamer Southern Cross. He says: Their tribal wars, cannibalism, head-hunting, and infanticide must also be added to the total as causes of extermination, but these have not nearly been so destructive as has•been the white man's pernicious influence. Let us see what is happening now. A great many of the pernicious influences have ceased to exist under the new order of things, and in parts of Melanesia the race is holding its own, and in some places, though, alas! not in very many, it is increasing. What is working, then, against the harmful influences to which we have referred? First and foremost, we must place tile missionary, who has been working on unsupported by any civil authority, and who , had to fight hard by protests addressed to the 1 civilised countries, which after many years have proved effectual. Owing to the protests made by missionaries the old pernicious class of trader is almost a thing of the past, and there is now a much better class of men engaged in trading and planting. The English Government, more- over, keeps a strict watch over recruiting, and interests itself in native affairs. It is sometimes asked, Are these natives ever likely to become of any use? Have they suffi- cient intelligence to learn and become valuable and useful British subjects? On this point I can speak with confidence, as it was my privilege, while in command of the mission vessel South- ern Cross, to train a native crew. There is only one way to treat these natives. If you wish to be successful you must' have un- limited patience, but, provided you are just and firm, they will become not only capable, but responsive, lovable, staunch, and honest to a degree. One has to bear in mind that the par- ents of some of the crew were cannibals, and even head-hunters, and have not had the cen- turies of Civilisation we have had to help to strengthen their characters. I am proud to say that our crew worked as well, and knew their work as well, as any English crew with which I ;have ever sailed. -Let me illustrate one part of their work by an incident which, occurred on ,board at the Solomon Islands. On our arrival at a certain place we found a British man-of-war at anchor and soon after we came to an anchor ourselves they started to signal to us in semaphore. I I called one of My native crew to stand by and read the signals, remaining with him, of course, and reading it myself also to be sure that he did not make any mistake. The native boy replied to all their questions in semaphore. The con- versation lasted about half an hour, and the last mc;s:saaigne saafuite:lar,s from the captain, inviting p_ae to come on, board to lunch. "That igsoat veryonsmarbtoasrigdnalmtahne you have; I suppose he is smile old man-of- war's think sow n man?" and on asking him why lie should .he signals so neatly and quickly; in " greeted with the reply, "Well, because e went so quickly it was hard to keep up v' 1 tlhim." Melanesian informednative f e that he iNlvaaLs ai been allowed to sign' a n I al in his own native lan- guage he would probably have gone so fast that no one would have been able to keep up with him at all. The spelling of the words in Eng- lish Was a bit of a trouble to him. Not only is that boy able to signal in semaphore, but all the native crew are able to do so.

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TIITLE-TATTLE.

Seventy-five in the shade. This was the record made in The heai; wave during the week. Still, there was a delightful breeze Rippling the/waters of the Ship Canal. "Heathens" gathered along the banks, And dreamt that they were at the seaside. The heat caused the tarred surface Of the roads to become liquid. Pedestrians carried the roadways away Attached to the soles of their boots. This caused the souls of the housewives Who like clean floors to be sadly vexed. Gardeners who use the canal water. Should be extremely careful, so one of them :hfnks. He bought a new water can for ss. At the first dip it slipped from His hands and sunk in the murky waters of he Canal. He now bewails the loss of can and ss.

Mrs. Brundrett's Will—The will of Mrs. Anna Maria Brundrett, th? highly-respected gtoekton Heath lady who died about three months ago, has been proved at ..t.BOB.

The Hay Harvest. —Farmers in the district have made rapid progress with the hay harvest. The crops are of a fair medium quality and quantity.

Thunderstorm.—The tropical heat of the pre- vious few clays culminated on Wednesday after- noon in one of the most severe thunderstorms experienced for some time. The lightning was vivid, but fortunately did not seem to have done any serious damage. Torrential rain followed and flooded some of the roads. The storm again re-visited the district on Thursday morning, but proved of brief duration.

Question of Mowing.—At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, the resolution of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee that attention be drawn to the bad state of the grass plot at the junction of Lewen- son's Brow" was again mentioned.—The Clerk: Attention was called to this about a year ago.— Mr. Swinton (surveyor): It is only a case of mowing the grass, and it is now being attended to.

11 %BRUCE OF MISS H. HUGHES. Solemnised at St. Thomas' Church.

At St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, the wedding was solemnised of Mr. Thomas War- burton, son of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Warburtod, of Rush Green, Lymm, and Miss Henrietta Hughes, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. C. H. Hughes, of Old London-road, Stockton Heath. The Rev. Tames Collins conducted the cere- mony in an impressive manner. The bride, who wore a charming dress of champagne satin, and hat of satin and tulle, trimmed with forget-me-note, carried a sheaf of lilies, and was given away by her father. The bridesmaids were the Misses May Hughes, Annie Evans, Edith Hughes and Ada War- burton, who wore dainty dresses of satin mer-e, with hats of black and white lace, trimmed with roses and forget-me-nots. Their bouquets were of pink carnations. The duties of best man were discharged by Mr. Albert Warburton, brother of the bride- groom. At the conclusion of the ceremony the couple left for the honeymoon in North Wales. The bride's travelling costume was of blue serge, with hat to match. Many useful presents were received.

ORFORD.

Death of Mr, W. E. A. Spencer.—The death has occurred of Mr. W. E. A. Spencer, of 127, Padgate-lane, after only a short illness. He came home from work on Thursday of last week and had to go to bed. On Friday the doctor pronounced him to be suffering from pneu- monia, and he passed away on Tuesday. He was a son of the late Vicar of Daresbury, and was in his 53rd year. Almost all his spare time was taken up with music. He was at one time assistant organist at St. Thomas', Stockton Heath, and for the last four years was choirmaster at Orford Church. His heart and soul were in his Church work, and he often talked about the choir during his illness. The funeral will take place this afternoon at the Warrington Cemetery, and a service will be hell in Orford Church. A memorial service will be held on Sunday in the same church.

GREAT SAN KEY.

Scholars' Successes.—The result of the Lan- cashire and Cheshire examination for the scholars of the Continuation .classes has just come to hand, and of the twelve who sat from Penketh four have passed with good percent- ages. They are Ivy Davies, George Houghton, Frank Finch and William White.

Gaming.—At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, William Harrison, 103, liramhall-street, Warrington, was fined 65., in- cluding costs, for gaming whilst walking be- tween Penketh and Sankey along the towing- path of the canal. Other defendants were dealt with at the previous Court and a warrant was issued for his arrest. He stated his previous non-attendance was 'due to a misunderstanding.

Girls' " American Tea."—The members of the Girls' Friendly Society (Sankey Branch) enter- tained about 100 ladies and gentlemen at an American tea, held 'at The Elms, the residence of Miss Tinsley, on Saturday afternoon. The object was to assist the fund being raised to purchase a font for the church, and also to contribute a small sum towards the Medical Mission. A splendid assortment of articles were for disposal upon stalls, in charge of the society assoeiates, and songs were rendered at intervals by Miss Moston and Miss Dodds, Miss Jennie Fairclough making an excellent lady palmist. Games of golf, quet. bowls, and quoits added to the interest of an enjoyable afternoon. • clock golf, cro-

PENK ETH. ASSAULT ON MARRIED WOMAN.

A Penketh Man Fined.

At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, before Mr. Aylmer Frost (in the chair) and Councillor Roberts, John Barlow, of Doe Green Farm, Penketh, was summoned' by Alice Blinkhorn, married woman, of Lock Cottages, Fiddler's Ferry, for assault: Mr. Arthur Browne appeared to prosecute and Mr. T. S. Steel to defend. For the prosecution it was stated that as Mr. and Mrs. Blinkhorn were walking arm-in-arm along the canal towing path, a Miss Thompson, who also lived in Lock Cottages, came along and very rudely pushed in between them. After Miss ThbThompson had referred to Mrs. Blinkhern as a "pig," nd a reply abut Thompson "looking like a piece of jelly tied in the middle." had been exchanged, it was alleged that the defendant, who was Thorap- a son's sweetheart, came along and struck Mrs. Blinkhorn on two occasions. He then struck the husband and was going to strike him a second time, when Miss Thompson interfered, telling him to strike the prosecutrix. Barlow's reply to this Was: "They are both as had as one another, and both ought to be in the canal." Complaints were afterwards made to the police.

• PROSECUTITIX FAINTS. The prosecutrix was placed in the witness box and after a few questions by Mr. Browne she fainted and had to be carried out of Court. On her return the Clerk (Mr. H. Greenall) asked if she would like the case adjourned, but she replied: "No, sir; go on with it." Mr. Steel, in cross-examination, elicited the fact that the parties were on friendly terms until recently, when trouble arose over a neigh- bones statement about Miss Thompson and the defendant. During the cross-examination Miss Thompson had to be assisted out of Court. The husband corroborated the evidence of his wife and said the nearest people to the scene were about 10 or 15 yards away. For the defence it was contended that Miss Thompson and the defendant met the prosecn- trix and her husband over an hour prior to the tioublo and nothing then passed between them. Later, as Miss Thompson was going to meet her young man, she met Mr. and Mrs. Blinkhorn, who, by moving from one side of the path to the other, refused to allow her to pass, where- upon she forced her way between the pair, who were linking. The defendant, who was going to meet Miss Thompson, saw all that took place, and after several utterances "stood up for his sweetheart," and hit Mr. Blinkhorn twice. Striking Mrs. Blinkhorn was denied. A couple of young men who witnessed the incident, were called and bore out the state- ment for the defence. The defendant, who stated he was a tanner on strike, was fined 10s. and costs.

LYMM.

LINES FROM LYAINL

Folk are thinking of holidays. The annual respite will soon Come round once more, and There will be the usual dash to the sea. 'Tis reported the " knuts " are already Studying the latest things in straw hats, And the " knutesses" the latest creations In summery frocks and hats. Gas was in evidence at the Council meeting. Eighteen new retorts are to be built. Will this prevent Lymm gas from Doing what was described as the "Tango Jump"? The Rector was congratulated on his marriage By his brother Councillors at Monday night's meeting. . It never rains but it pours says the motto. In the matter of congratulations the Rector must think the motto is very true. Lymm is threatened with a motor 'bus service. A company sought power to run such vehicles Through the streets of the village to Latchford. The Council decided that they had no power To grant permission—the County Council are' the people to do so. Many would welcome a motor 'bus service to Warrington. The train service is not all that could be desired. The "Terriers" went to church on Sunday. The martial strains of the band caused The annual thrill of excitement in the village. The "gallant youths" looked very smart, And the young ladies admired the red tunics, So all were pleased with the annual church Parade. Benedicts in a solid mass now rule Lymm. According to Councillor Warburton, the Dis- trict Council Chairman, Now the Rector has got married there

Is not a single man upon the Council. A good subject for the Debating Societies next season Would be: Is Ly-mm better governed by Married men than it would be by bachelors? This benedict Council of Lymin recalls the conundrum: If an empty ship lay at a quay and Fifty married couples went on, how Many would be on board that ship? The answer is "Not a single person."

Footpath Cyclists.—At the meeting of the Dis- trict Council on Monday, Councillor Gotobed stated that he had seen no improvement in the footpath cycling nuisance.

Gas Works.—At a recent meeting of the General Purposes Committee Of the Lymm Dis- trict Council it was resolved that the Clerk obtain £l,OOO by way of loan on the security of the Gas Works.

Gas Retorts.—The District Council on Monday gave their approval to the recommendation of the Gas Committee to accept the tender of Cammack Dennis to supply and fix, 18 gas retorts for £llO.

The Village Fishermen.—The District Council on Monday agreed to the recommendation of the Water Committee that in addition to the scale of charges already passed for fishing in the lake, tickets be issued to residents at 6d. for a day and is. for a weekly card.

Another Sub-committee.—On Monday the District Council agreed to the recommendation of the General Purposes Committee that a Sewage Farm Sub-committee be appointed to consist of the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston), and Councillors Groves, Lockyer, Kershaw and Parker.

Whole Council Committee.—The District Councillors at the meeting on Monday adopted the resolution of the Housing and Town Plan, fling Committee that a committee be appointed to consider adoption of the Public Health Amendment Act, 1907, and that the whole of the members of the Council are to constitute the committee.

Another Pump.—The question of pumping at the 'Sewage Works was discussed at the meeting of the District Council en Monday evening, when Mr. Beckett, the expert retained by the Council. attended.—After a very long discus- sion it was decided that Mr. Beckett should purchase another pump, the cost not to exceed the estimated ..t6O.

Present.—At the meeting of the District Council on Monday evening there were present Councillor T. H. Warburton (chairman), Councillor F. Lockyer (vice-chairman), Rev. G. B. Thurston, Councillors Groves, Gotobed, Ker- shaw, Parker, Holt, Critchley, and Cowper, to- gether with Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk), Barnes (water works manager), Mort (surveyor), and Dr. De Jong (medical officer). Sewage Works Foreman.—When the minutes of the Sanitary Committee of the District Coun- cil were read at Monday night's meeting, Coun- cillor Kershaw moved their confirmation, with the exception of the one in favour of appoint- ing Mr. Edward Hazlehurst as foreman of the new sewage farm, at the salary of 235. per week. The matter, he proposed, should be referred back, and after a long discussion this was agreed to.

Croquet Match.—The Lymm croquet team entertained the Northern on Saturday, when the match was decided on handicap instead of opens, so as to equalise the sides. The visitors won by three matches to one.

_ Culvert Near the Old Mill.—The Lymm Dis- trict Council on Monday confirmed the minute of the Roads Committee, which directed the Clerk to write to Mr. Clarke, agent for the Lymm Estate Development Co., and say that unless the necessary repairs to the culvert at tie mill were commenced within a week from June 27th, the Council would have the culvert filled up.—Mr. Mort (the surveyor) was !in- structed to make the necessary arrangements. Clerical Assistance.—lt was stated at the meeting of the District Council on Monday that at the last meeting of the General Purposes Committee the Surveyor gave a report of his duties as Surveyor and Nuisance Inspector, and it. was resolved that a clerk be advertised for at 15s. per week, who would devote four days a week to the Surveyor and two days to the Clerk. It was stated ‘that the advertisement had ap- peared, and that there was only one applicant, Mr. C. Mort, who was appointed by the Council to the position. Meter 'Bus Service.—At the meeting of the District Council on Monday a letter was read' from the British Automobile Traction Co., Ltd., asking the Council to favourably consider the application for motor 'bus licences to connect Lymm with Latchford and Knutsford. The firm purpose continuing their Macclesfield—, Knutsford service as far as Latehford, and so serve the population of Lymm and High Legh. If the application was favourably received it was the intention to start the service immedi- ately, for everything was in order for com- mending, on Saturday.—The Clerk said he be- lieved they had no power in the matter; it was a matter for the County Council, and it was decided that a reply to that effect should be sent.

Sunday School Anniversary.—The Congrega- tional Sunday school anniversary will take place to-morrow. The preacher will he the Rev. B. Ness, of Zion Institutional Church, Man- chester.

Registratmn.—The Liberal Association Com; mittee would be glad to hear from any Liberals who have just come into the district, also of any who wish to claim lodger votes. The hon. secretary of the Association is Mr. E. Barker, Oaklea, Statham, Lymm.

COUNCIL "A MASS OF MARRIED MEN.'

The members of the District Council, on Monday, through the Chairman (Councillor Warburton) added their good wishes to the Rector and Mrs. Thurston's future. Councillor Warburton said that was the first time since he had been connected with the Council that they were "a mass of married men.' Now, continued the Chairman, we feel we are a happy band. The Rector has taken a big re- sponsibility upon his shoulders, but we believe he has made the right choice, because we all know it is not every woman who can make a minister's wife. His work will be lessened to a large extent, and he will feel the benefit of that before long. The Rector thanked them very 'much for the kind remarks, and trusted that the domestic felicity of which the Chairman had spoken would help him to be a better attender at the Council meetings. He hoped his marriage would be no bar to his duties on the Council.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPAIR OF A ROAD. Mr. Wathin and "_A Matter of Supreme

Indifference."

At the meeting of the District Council on Monday it was stated that at a meeting of the Roads Committee a letter was read from Mr. Watkin with reference to the road opposite Danebank House, and the Clerk was then directed to reply and say " the Council re- gretted to not the tone of the letter, but the road would be taken in hand as soon as pos- sible." A letter in reply to that of the Council's Was read at Monday's meeting. It ran "Danebank, Lymm.—To the Clerk.—Sir,—l am directed by my father to reply to your letter of 25th June, re the repairing of road opposite Danebank. I wish to emphasise the fact that it is a matter of supreme indifference to Mr. Watkin what the Council think of the tone of his letter. On August 29th, 1913, you wrote and sfated that 'the Council had made arrange.. ments with Mr. Melville to repair the portion of the road referred to.' On March 27th, 1914, Mr. Watkin received a letter from Mr. Mel- - stating that 'in conjunction with the purchase of additional land for sewage works extension the price agreed to be paid included an amount for the permanent repair and main- tenance by the Council of the roadway opposite Danebank. I might say I have used every endeavour to have this work carried out, and , any further delay is through no cause of mine.' "In your letter of June 25th, 1914, you state that 'what has been done is only temporary.' Such delay on the part of the Council is dis- graceful. "The Council did not hesitate to use Mr. •Watkin's name in a Parliamentary Committee Room, but seem unable to carry out their duty by him.—Yours truly, "HUGH G. WATKIN:'

"P.S.—Kindly state the date when the per- manent repair of the road is to be commenced and completed." A Member: Least said soonest mended.

MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL. Successful Termination to " Hustled " Preparations.

Whilst the " hustling " committee of the Lymm May Queen Festival were making their hurried preparations there were well-wishers who doubted the wisdom of the venture and Predicted a financial loss. However, all fears were set at rest on Tuesday evening, when the balance sheet was presented to the committee at a meeting at the Fox Tavern. The "speeding up" process was carried through right up to the end, for the balance sheet had been drawn up under a month, and, taking into considera- tion the number of small accounts, it spoke well for the efforts of the Treasurer (Mr. W. C. Jones), and he was specially thanked for his work. The financial statement showed that about .2140 was received from all sources, which, when the entire expenditure was deducted, left a handsome balance in hand of .0.6 165., and from this the committee unanimously decided to vote a sum sufficient to wipe off the deficit existing since the 1912 festival, and to carry the r'emaining balance forward to next year. The committee decided to hold a children's tea and sports day, when all who took part in the procession will be invited. The date was provisionally fixed for July 15th, and a sub- committee was appointed to deal with the matter.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES OF THANKS.

The committee passed special votes of thanks to Mrs. Houghton for presenting the Queen's bouquet; Miss Houghton for making tie pre- sentation; Mr. Richards for gift of hidden treasure cake; Mr. Egerton for gift of the lamb; Altrincham Section of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, and the Boy Scouts for giving ambu- lance displays when the committee were dis- appointed by the non-appearance, owing to sickness, of the Lancashire Lasses' troupe of dancers; and to Mrs. Walker, of Fox Tavern, who gave free use of the room for the meetings. The Chairman (Mr. Hudson) and the Hon. Secretary (Mr. Webb, jun.) were also thanked for their services.

THE SECRETARY'S THANKS. We have received a letter from the Hon. Secretary, in which he states: "Will you please allow me, through the mediuin of your paper, to express, on behalf of the officers and com- mittee of Lymm May Queen Festival, our sin- cere thanks to all those friends who either directly or indirectly so kindly helped us on Whit-Thursday, and to whom it is impossible to write individually."

RECTOR'S "A'l' HOME."

The I,lllagers Entertained at the Rector.

COUNCILLORS' AND " TERRIERS' " TRIBUTES. A goodly 'number of guests assembled at the Rectory to meet Mrs. Thurston at a series of "at homes" on Wednesday and Thursday. The grounds were very tastefully decorated, but unfortunately rain prevented full enjoy- ment of the same. The wedding presents were on view in two of the rooms, and were greatly admired by the guests. A string band gave musical selections, and refreshments were provided in a marquee on the lawn.

It must have been very gratifying to Mr, and Mrs. Thurston to see so great evidence of the respect which the parishioners of Lymm have for their Rector. No man could enter ',upon married life with heartier wishes for his and his wife's future happiness and prosperity than Mr. Thurston has had expressed to him from all sources.

BAND IN RECTORY GROUNDS. The villagers focussed their attention on the Rectory grounds on Sunday afternoon, where, through the kindness of Lieut.-Colonel Groves, the Band of the sth (Earl of Chester) Battalion, Cheshire Regiment, played numerous selections. The Rector introduced Mrs. Thurston to a large number of the visitors.
