===== 0002601_19140704_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
A CANADIAN HOLIDAY,

Mr. B. W. Atkinson Sees filature at its

Best.

NOVEL EXPERIENCES.

Mr. R. W. Atkinson, son of Mr. and Mrs. J. Atkinson (Atkinson's Cafes, Bridge-street), who is stationed at Brandon, Manitoba, wfiere he is deputy borough treasurer, has been in Canada for two years, and is doing remarkably well. Originally, he went to fulfil an engagement at Winnipeg, and afterwards became secretary to the Member of Parliament for Brandon.

_ He recently took a holiday, and a long letter written home gives a vivid description of the resources of a new country in this respect. We give a few extracts from Mr. Atkinson's account of a trip through the wilds of Ontario by canoe, with a party consisting of Mr. and Mrs. Sykes, Ralph Sykes, aged five, Mr. W. H. Green and himself. _ _ _

Deciding to go through the Quetico Forest reserve and game preserve, including a series of lakes and streams, they set out with tents, blankets, rugs, fishing tackle, cameras and other necessities, tobacco and matches completing a long list of comforts. . Arriving near the flag station, Windigo, May 14, by the side of the lake, they immediately loaded up canoes and started on the voyage of discovery. The trip was one long varied ex- perience of travelling by water and making short cuts overland to save time and labour, the conditions being of the most primitive kind. We make the following, selections from the letter :

and rabbits. However, there's lots of timber there for it to chew at, so let it live.

A FISHERMAN'S PARADISE.

„ ....... After steady paddling down the prettiest lake of all our journey, we it last reached our destination Eden Island, Quitico Lake, at 1.30 p.m., on the 20th May, after going a fair aver- age of 15 miles 9 day, which isn't bad time at all for novices, such; as we were. The superin- tendent smilingly wrote us out and signed a certificate to the effect that we had reached the aforesaid place at 1.30 p.m. on the 20th day of May, A.D. 1914. Well, we fixed our camp up, and had just got the pan on the stove, when we. had a terrific thunderstorm, and we were very thankful that canvas was covering us. The rain was so heavy that it left a heavy mist over the ;water, so that one could not see the lake fur- ther than about 20 yards from shore. Thursday, 215t.--After brekker two of us went down the lake to Quetico Dam and fished, and here we caught some big fish. Our biggest was a lake trout, weighing 1.61 b. and 'measuring 40in. in length and 19'in. gii'th. When we got home for dinner Sykes had made a log table and two seats about sft. long, placed them in front of the big tent, so that we could now eat comfort- ably. For the remainder of the day we lazied about and played rummy. We didn't feel like doing any mare fishing. As it was we gave the rangers all our fish with the exception of an 81b. trout, which we had for supper done up in steaks. We find trout is by far the best this way. The following morning we all went down to Queticlo Dam and had a grand day's sport fishing. We caught so many fish that we threw Ithem all back as we landed them, with the ex- ception of enough for a couple of meals.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST WHITE WOMAN SEEN SINCE NOVEMBER.

Friday morning, 15th.—Up early and had just finished breakfast on trout, when we heard a motor in the distance, and shortly afterwards the Superintendent of the reserve, along. with three rangers, landed on the point. They had a 2-h.p. motor in a canoe. We needn't remark how very glad we were to see them, and espe- cially when we found we were on the right track, and having a good chat and receiving directions from them, we went on. They were going up to the rail for stores. We crossed the last portion of the lake and made our way down French River, which was exceedingly pretty; arrived at oar first decent-sized portage and had a meal. Here two/of us walked across the portage to the headquarters of the forest ran- gers, and presented a note from the superin- tendent there, and one of the men immediately embarked with a team and sleigh to bring our canoes and duffle and the remainder of the party across, for this portage was over two miles long, and we were very thankful that a lift over, without trouble, was available. Here at headquarters we found .the superintendent's wife, who welcomed the lady of our party pro- fusely, as she was the first white woman she had seen since November, 1913. We had to wait here until the return of the superintendent, so we put up the tents and spent a lazy time talk- ing to the rangers and mooching around. Our quarry arrived home after supper, and we had a, very interesting talk with him, during which time he fixed us up with another map, our fish- ing permits, and directions so numerous that ,we at last had to discard them and trust to our own maps and good sense, for we never. could agree when it came to going one of two ways, which one he had directed us to follow. For, of course, the maps one gets hold of are far from correct, and the compass is one's only guide half the way.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
NEARLY LOST.

Pickerel • was our first big lake. About 20 miles in length, and from two to five miles in Width, one can imagine what it Would be like on a windy day, but luckily we struck a fair day and had no trouble. We threw out two lines as we paddled along, and very soon got our dinner in the form of two grey trout about 41b. each. After paddling for a straight six hours,and going through the biggest portion of Pickerel Lake, which was studded with num- bers of small and large islands, all thickly wooded, we camped on a rocky island in the middle of the lake, about five miles from the bottom of it. Here'we had our meal, and I had a swim, but it was cold. I took a header off a rock, and wasn't in above a minute, and when I got out I was tingling to beat the band. Still, I felt fine after it and ate a hearty meal. At five we went on again until 6.45, and landed, well, we couldn't decide just where, and we began to think that after all, we were going to get lost, as was prophesied, in Pickerel Lake. However, we camped there for the night, and as the following day was Sunday we decided to stay there until Monday morning. We had a little difficulty in finding • a suitable camping ground owing to the rocky shore, but after a little search we spotted one, and made our usual bed. During the night we were worried by some enormous mosquitos, "but Greenie had a fixed battle with them for a while and freed us from the annoyance. Weather so far very hot, sky perfectly clear, and everybody feeling grand. All of us beginning to look like Indians. Sunday we spent a lazy &Y.

LET IT LIVE! Tuesday morning, 19th,.—Up early and made • more bread, and left at 11.40, paddling down McAlpine against a strong head wind to a little stream, which was very pretty, leading into Kasakokwog Lake. illere we had a small port- age of 66 yards, which finally landed us in Kasakokwog. Crossing this lake we had an awful time of it. The lake was very rough to begin with, and the wind kept coming up in squalls, so that we had to stick close in to flie bank, and.twice had to pull in shore and shelter from a. little shower of rain. Still we made the lake's ten miles in good time, and after a short .search foulid the stream leading to our last portage, which was to bring us into Quetico Lake. After paddling down a very narrow stream, we arrived at the portage. We found an old log cabin on ' the hill and had quite a little excitement in there. Green and I had paddled down stream a little further than the others to find the proper trail; and we heard a • yell, to which we, of course, instantly re- sponded: Arriving at the log hut we saw Sykes inside standing watching a peculiar animal calmly chewing at the leg of an old table, wondering whether it would tackle him or not. I ran to the canoe for the revolver and camera, I the two articles for shooting, and went up to the cabin again, where the gruesome animal began to strut around the floor, presumably scared, and trying to get out. It took us quite a long time to decide what it was; but at last we fixed on a porcupine. The reason we couldn't see at first that it was a porcupine was that, instead of having long quills it was all hairy, and, as we found out later from the rangers, it was too early in the season for the full back of quills. Still, it was a wopper, and I took two photos of it and then we chased it out of the hut. Let me remind you that we were in a flame preserve, otherwise we should have shot it, for they are very destructive beggars. We were rather riled when .we arrived at Quetico and saw the rangers 'again, that we had not done so, for they told us they had instructions to kill all pests, such as porcupine, fox, skunk

===== 0002601_19140704_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINOS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST —(continued).

induced so much as to taste any other. She brought a lot of it with her to the seaside, and spent a happy quarter of an hour or so in set- ting forth precise instructions as to the brewing of the precious beverage. I told my wife to dis- regard the injunction, and at least for that day to substitute our own tea. Auntie sipped this with evident enjoyment, at the same time con- gratulating us on the rare taste which she had acquired, and especially on her forethought in making such ample provision of the incompar- able tea. "Come nor," said she, "confess that there is none like it." "I agree," said I, " that your's is very good tea indeed; but I will not admit that it is superior to this." Then.l told the brutal truth, wondering what sort of whig- ging I should get; but to my relief the sensible old lady joined heartily in the laugh against her- self, and said, " Ah, well, so much for imagina- tion.".

===== 0002601_19140704_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
INDIAN GRAVES.

We made a few trips and in the course of our wanderings spotted some Indian graves. The Indian graves are very quaint. Some of the tribes build up poles and lay the body on the ' top to decay, and we saw numerous edifices. Of course, they return and bury the skeleton. Other tribes bury them and erect a small box affair over the grave and cover them up with beautiful bead mats. Also all kinds of articles are laid there; such as one we saw contained a small pair of mocassins, a model birch-bark canoe and paddles, two matches and a minia- ture bundle of wood, a piece of plug tobacco and a pipe. The idea of putting all these things there is the Indian idea that when the departed one returns to life, they will have something to start on. This isn't a very good way of ex- plaining it, but it gives you the idea any way. On Monday morning, at 11, Greenie and I set off on our way home. Instead of going back we were en route for Kettle Falls, practically due west as the crow flies. Well, we got to the end of Quetioo Lake and portaged over the dam into Beaverhouse Lake (ten miles long), and here we hit up against a very strong head wind.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
A NASTY UPSET.

. - - We took the north course of the river and made three small and rough portages over small falls, and we were so sick of portaging that when we came to a rapid a little further down stream, we decided to run them, so, hey, ho! we ran them fine until we got to the end, and it turned suddenly to the left, and the river pre- cipitated itself over a Mt. fall, and before We knew where we were we found ourselves in the water and the canoe floating along upside down. We made bank easily, and found Billie had hurt his knee On a rock. I had very fortunately kept still until I was in calm water and missed all the rocks. So it was up to me to swim after the canoe. It was cold. However, after a swim of about 20 yards I caught up the canoe and pushed it ashore, turned it over, and behold there was all the duffle inside as safe as a cucumber. Didn't I yell with delight. I threw all the duffle on ' shore and swam after the paddles, and then :tried to turn the canoe over to empty the water out and couldn't. Billie couldn't swim across because of his knee (he tried twice), and we were at a loss what to do, until bailing occurred to us; so I bailed with the cooklpot and then paddled across for Billie. Then for two hours we had our duffle arrayed along with ourselves on a rock drying. Of course we lost our matches and the bread was soaked and useless. All our other grub was too wet to use, and would be useless when dry, so we didn't know what to do, whether to go back to Quetico Lake for fresh supplies, or whether to chance going hungry until we reached Kettle Falls. Eventually we decided that if We went back, we should have to go hungry for a day, and also we might have to rob the Sykes of necessary stores, so we dried out, and with a small bag of rice, and a small chunk of bacon, as our supply of food, we set off once more, this time portaging all the rapids and falls we came to.

NATURE'S OWN. We embarked on the Brandon train and ar- rived home once more at midnight, or rather' early morning; Sunday, the 31st May, -after ;l enSe :el 1,1 air tbhye AM m ehatilvi ei having and t r ispo obfy ogyeesi6o most beautiful country and the wildest I hal4 ever seen. The canoe trip was mostly all through absolutely virgin country, through which only trappers, rangers, and Indians, and an occasional tripper, have gone. Mrs. Sykes is the first white woman to take such an exten- sive trip through that country, and that in itself is ample evidence of its wildness. - • , Nature seems to excel itself in beauty in this magnificent country. Not one spot in the whole journey was it bare. There wasn't even a' patch bigger than 20 feet square which wasn't thickly wooded, and tha,solitude—well, it, speaks for itself. Not a sound except for the crying of the birds, the call of a moose, or the bark of a wolf, and the splashing of the fish as they rise for the flies. We often raised a cry when we got to a high rocky bank, and the echo was wonderful. It seemed to echo and re-echo again, until it died down inta a slight sound, hardly distinguishable. We often rested on our paddles as we.saw some fresh beauty in Nature, and often one would express a thought on the magnificent loveliness of the work of Nature. The water in the lakes and rivers was as pure as gold. When one felt thirsty, a cup over the side of the canoe and a dip in the lake, and one had the most refreshing drink of beautiful water in the world. The whole country is bursting full with • all kinds of fruits. Oh, what it must be like to be there in the autumn! If We ever get the opportunity to visit that country again we intend going at the end ,of Aurst. At that time the flies and mosquitoes are few, fishing and bathing are good, the weather isn't too Warm, and all the fruits are out. In conclusion, I must say that the whole trip was one full of interest and excitement, and was c'ertainly one of the finest holidays I have ever spent or ever wish to spend. It is inexpensive, the whole trip didn't cost us over 35 dollars', Fhich, out here, is a paltry figure for a, holi- day of 17 days. To be down there in the per- fect peace and solitude of Nature's own is be- yond all words of expression. It makes one feel 'ls if he were right iii heaven.

EX AM

INER, SATURDAY, JULY 4, 1914.

AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHY.

Suggestions for Making Seaside Work Successful.

HOLIDAY WORK.

More plates and films are spoilt at the sea- side during the holidays by over-exposure than by all the other faults usually attributed to the plate or to ,the camera-shutter, lens, or tripod. By this error the beginner often brings home a crop of fiat, thin negatives that can never be made to produce pictures. And yet no place is more favourable to good camera work than the seaside, for it must be a poor lens that will not work at F. 22. And that is quite a wide enough stop for marine snap-shots. It is strange how a beginner will over-expose. He seems to think that the Click of a shutter is so quickly, made that he is not getting enough for his money, and so must have a time exposure, or something of the kind. , Three important "don'ts" for the seaside are: Don't use too rapid a plate, too slow a shutter, or too large a stop. As to subjects. If I saw a cad stalking any friends of mine who were bathing or wad- ing, in order to take his "ripping" views, unless he, too, was known to his 'subjects, that picture box would some way or other be knocked out of his hand and stamped on, and I'd risk the consequences, On the other hand, an ex- ception must be made in the case of children. To secure the most natural and pleasing pic- tures, the little ones must be snapped unawares. , Again, to try to hide a camera whilst taking 'unrehearsed pictures is not much use nowadays, ! when no one can mistake a, camera for a tele- scope or a luncheon basket.' The art of taking snapshots unawares is not so much in disguis- ing the instrument as in the skill of the worker. That is why the fixed focus camera is so handy. I was out with a man once, wha, in —, saw a splendid group at a cottage door, and if he didn't actually run to his bicycle to unstrap came back, of course, every treryoili7edolf Wetheillittle untidy urchins had gone, and he was upset for an hour or two at his ill- luck. I didn't do any photography then. The very first camera journey I ever took led me down the Wye Valley. I was at Tin- tern Abbey, and had gone up to a certain win- dow to obtain a favourable view. Whilst doing the necessary adjusting I noticed two awful." toffs" enter the area below, and waited for a clear space. But they took good care to let me hear the " whisper ": "Yes, they're everywhere; you can't enjoy anything, any- where, for them !" And "I'd never said a single word !" _ _

And everybody who knows me knows also what an unobtrusive chap I am,

_ But, again! On that same journey my com- panion, having obtained for us from the Dean of a written permit to photograph in the cathedral, actually went within the communion rail of the chancel and fixed his camera up there. Is there any wonder that the verger was on him in a moment and subjected him to the indignity of being ordered out. "I'm per- fectly sure, sir, the Dean would not consent to your standing there," was all he said. No, we might to be gentlemen, even if we possess a camera, and think whether our pleasure causes annoyance to strangers before doing what, per- haps, we should be the first to object to, were the tables turned.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
SNA PSI-I OTS. '

Mr. W. T. Wilkinson, in the" 8.J.," writes: "In the year 1854 I stood for my first photo- graph in a backyard; the photograph was on glass, quarter-plate size, black-varnished on the film side, and was delivered without any ffame price 10s. 6d." A good motto for a picture-maker is: "When in doubt leave it out." When we Cannot decide whether any part of a view is advantageous to the composition or not, the chances are that its omission will add strength to the picture .and make it more worth the trouble of recording. Half a dozen objects claiming our notice can- not receive as much attention as would be given to half as many. Nature is so lavish with her menu that our pictures are apt to suffer the indigestion of the glutton. "Plain living and high thinking have made many a great man." SILVER-STREET.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of hiteresting

Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

The freighter Santa Cruz arrived recently at ' San Francisco with a new record for the passage by way of the Straits of Magellan. From the time the vessel left Sandy Hook till she slowed down off Los Angeles harbour, the engines were run at full speed without a stop, and tho dis- tance of about 13,000 miles was made in 47 days and 4 hours. The Santa Cruz is an oil burner, and therefore did not need to put in any port for fuel.

The Soo and the Suez Canals Compared. William C. Redfield, secretary of commerce, in a recent address, drew attention to the fact that the number of ships passing through the river and canal connecting Lake Huron and Lake Superior amounted last year to nearly three times the number that passed through the Suez Canal. To be exact, 14,916 vessels of 30,974,123 tons passed through the Great Lakes Canal, while 5,300 vessels of 20,275,133 tons passed through the Suez Canal.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
Wireless Waves and Birds.

Observations made in sections of the world where there are many wireless stations indicate that birds are disturbed in a singular way by the wireless waves. It is stated that gulls are apparently the principal sufferers, but that also large numbers of doves are in some way pre- vented from finding their way home when there are wireless stations in the line of flight. This strange phenomenon is attributed to some effect of the ether waves not yet understood.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Beware of the Canary.

Medical men are warning those who have feathered pets to take measures for their disin- fection, especially with the sudden burst of warm days. It is pointed out that even the dainty canary, the last thing held by its owner to be the subject of suspicion, is unable with- out help to free its 'down from unwelcome animal life. Therefore plenty of water for its morning bath, with a disinfecting solution for germicide carefully added, is necessary.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
A 1,800 Years Old Egg.

. . An egg which cannot be said to be fresh is one which dates from 1,800 years back. It was found during recent excavations made at Niko- polis, in the tomb of an 11 months old child, Sextius Rufus. Already more than 80 tombs hete been explored by the.archteological service in this ancient city, which was built to com- memorate the battle of Actium. Among the finds are a quantity of gold jewels, also Pottery and lamps ornamented with elegant figures in relief.

Dissipating Fog by Wireless. The North Railroad Company in France is making experiments on the use of wireless waves for clearing away fog. It is well known that electric waves act upon the water particles which make up fog, so as to dissolve them and turn them into vapour. Following this idea, it is claimed that as much as 600 feet can be cleared up in front of the electric wires which are sending out the waves, the fog being at least Partially dissipited, and this Will have a great value in practice, especially for railroads and ships.

Australian Coal Trade's Growth.

The Australian coal trade for the quarter end- ing March 31st last shows a big increase in ex- ports, especially to foreign countries. The ex- ports were 1,365,000 tons, as compared with 1,047,000 for the first quarter of 1913. Of these totals, there were just under 600,000 tons ex- ported to foreign countries, as compared with 344,000 tons in the March quarter of last year. , The biggest increase is in the Far East, a quar- ter in which Australian trade all round has in recent years made strong advances.

Hardening Steel with Compressed Air. The usual methods of hardening steel in oil, Water, and other special baths produce a great tension along ,the line of contact between the hardened and the unhardened parts. A recent German improvement on this process is' the use of a number of jets of compressed air play- ing on the hot steel. The zones of cooling are graduated beautifully by varying the number and spacing of the openings in the nozzle. A wide range of results is possible by aciapting the shape of the nozzle to that of the work.

Floating on the Dead Sea. The wonderful buoyancy of the Dead Sea, that strange inland sheet of water in Palestine, is proverbial. It is some 47 'miles long by 9 miles wide, and lies no less than 1,200 feet below the surface of the _Mediterranean, the lowest-lying lake on the face of the globe. Its waters are so bitter that fish cannot live in them. We get an idea of its density when it is stated that in a ton of water from the Atlantic there is 31 pounds of salt against 187 pounds from a like quan- tity in the Dead Sea. The result is that it is impossible for a human being to sink in these waters.

Ostrich Feather Plucking. During the 'course of the sitting of the Dominions Royal Commission at Port Elizabeth, the question of the supposed, cruelty attending the plucking of ostrich feathers, as affecting the market, was raised, when a witness, Mr. Evans, stated that while in London he delivered a lec- ture with lantern slides showing the method of plucking. After the lecture a lady toldshim she had not bought feathers for three years on account of. the cruelty she supposed was in- volved in the plucking, but after the explana- tion which had been given, she expressed her in- tention to purchase some next day.

The Coming Country. There is a general belief that the Argentine is the coming country, and this is verified in the eyes of the Londoner by the fact that the smart- est London stores now add Buenos Ayres to their lists of local habitations—London, Paris, and Buenos Ayres being the fashionable trading places of the super-shops of the world. The South American is now beginning to occupy the chief suites in the hotels that used to be the province of the North American, and in the competition for social favours the newly rich Of the two nations are now found eagerly corn- 'peting for the same things.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Pharaoh's Serpents.

A cone of mercuric sulpho-cyanate, when ig- nited, burns with the development of a tre- mendous amount of ash. The cone seems to swell and writhe away like a serpent, hence the name. Any boy can make the cones at little expense. To a solution of corrosive sublimate, add a few drops of ferric chloride. Dissolve potassium sulpho-cyanate in water, and slowly add this to the other solution, until, after stir- ring. a red colour remains. Fikr through a sheet of muslin and forth the white curds into little balls or cones. Dry, and you need only touch a match to produce Pharaoh's serpents. Do not breathe the vapours.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
DECREASED CRIME IN CHESHIRE.

The Cheshire Quarter Sessions were held at Chester Castle on Afonday, before the Chairman (Sir Horatio Lloyd) and a full Bench of magis- trates. In charging the Grand Jury, he said the calendar contained the names of ten prison- ers, against fifteen this time last year, and at Knutsford there was a similar decrease, which was very satisfactory. At a subsequent meet- ing of the county justices, Mr. J. F. T. Royds was appointed. assessor for the purpose of the , Clergy Discipline Act, in place of the late Colonel France-Hayhurst.

OUR COMPETITION.

My dear Children,— The entries for the competition, which closed last week-end, were not so numerous as I had honed for, but the reason for this is not far to seek. The fine weather had tempted you to spend so much of your time out of doors that you had not the time to write letters. Isn't that so?

The letters sent in, however, are very interest- ing, and show how a first-class holiday can be spent in the vicinity of Warrington. I have doubt every one of you can find means of ,enjoyment near your homes without going to the seaside or other holiday resorts, and the letters indicate that such is the case. Our prize goes to:

Aquatania, set sail on her maiden voyage to New York. I spent a very happy day, and enjoyed myself immensely. I then caught the 9.30 train from Liverpool, and, tired and sleepy, I reached Warrington safely.—Your nephew, FRED SHELLEY, 85, Winwick-road.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGGIE RANDLE'S, 329, WELLFIELD-STREET.

I must congratulate this niece on the way she spent her holiday. She made use of the morn- ing to do housework, and then, in the after- noon, had some -fine fun with the ducks on the pond.

The other letters were likewise of interest, and below I give a selection from them. All the competitors are sincerely thanked for their efforts, and I hope they will be successful in future competitions.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
Your loving UNCLE JACK.

WORK AND PLAY.

Dear Uncle Jack.—During our week's holiday we had rather nice weather, and I am sure all the children enjoyed themselves. I did. I did not go away, but I enjoyed myself at home just as well as being away. In the mornings I helped my grandmother to do the housework, but in the afternoon I went into our garden to look after the ducks. We have a large duck pond at the bottom of the garden, and the ducks have some fine times swimming in it. I sat on the grass watching them swim, and I am pleased to tell you I found it a very pleasant occupation. They would duck their heads right under the water, and then like a shot they would again lift them up. All round the pond they have made large holes with their big bills. Every now and then they would come out of the pond for a rest and something to eat, and then, when ready, they would toddle back for some more sport in the water. We have got some ducks that are only a week old, and the pretty litt4 things look so small when they are in the water with the others. Dear Uncle Jack, I think I have told you how I spent my holiday, hoping you enjoyed yours.—Your loving niece, MAGG IE RANDLES,, 329, Wellfield-street.

HELPED HER MOTHER.

Dear Uncle Jack,-1 enjoyed my holidays very much, especially Whit Monday. On that day I went with my mother .to my aunty's. We started at three o'clock, and I loved to watch the birds twittering in the trees. Mother and I then called in Victoria Park, where I saw somo gentlemen having a game of bowls on the bowl- ing green. I also saw children romping about on the grass. I would have liked to have joined in their fun, but we had not time. We stayed at aunty's till it was dusk, and then we proceeded on our way home. All the other days I helped my mother to keep the house tidy and com- fortable.—Your loving niece,

MONA ARCHER (age 11), \ 11, Heathside.

WENT BEAUTIFUL WALKS. Dear am very glad to find that •there is another Competition to answer. During the Whitsuntide holidays I went for beautiful walks in the country, and also helped my mother. As I walked down Bewsek I saw some boys fishing, and I stayed and watched them a little while. As I approached the duck pond I saw many 'small ducks and also some big ones. - Acre I threw some bread in the water for the ' chicks, so that they might have a good feed. J. walked on and saw many butterflies flittering about, and the birds were busy flying about and singing their sweet songs. I plucked many wild flowers and pretty leaves. After a little rest on the grass I made ,my way botneward, ready for my tea. Hoping you enjoyed your holiday as well as I did.---Your loving niece, HILDA PALMER, 3, Worsley-street.

A RIVER RESCUE AT RUNCORN. t,

Thomas Jones, of Oakland-street', Widnes, rescued a boy from the Mersey at Widnes, on Sunday evening. He was standing on the Transporter Bridge approach, When he was in- formed that a boy had got into the water. Without hesitation he rushed to the promenade wall and went after the •boy, who *as being carried away by the strong . ebb tide. Be reached the boy without 'difficulty, and --suc- ceeded in bringing him to the side. Artificial respiration was resorted to and he recored from his exhaustion. The boy. Thomas Ben- tham, Dock-street, was afterwards taken home.

A record was created by the departures from Liverpool to the Isle of Man on Saturday, the number of passengers by the steamers being • some thousands in excess of Any previous total for a Saturday in June.

WOMAN SUFFRAGE.

The Premier and the East End Women Workers. M. ASQUITH SYMPATHETIC.

BY A LADY CONTRIBUTOR.

The most striking of recent events has been Mr. Asquith's reception of a deputation of the East London suffragists. It consisted of five working women, headed by Mrs. John Scurr. The simple words in which they told the hard story of their lives was most impressive. In the East End it is common for working ..romen. to earn is. or Bs. for a full week's work, and it is impossible for a woman with such a miserable pittance to live decently. Mrs. Hughes, an elderly brush-maker, handed the Prime Minister a hairbrush, and told him "1 work from eight in the morning to six at night and get twopence for each brush. That brush is sold for half a guinea. I think I have a right to a vote the same as my husband, who hardly does any work, while I have to do the housework as well."

Mr. Asquith handled the brush and asked the woman, " How many hours does it take you to

make it?"

"About two hours," she replied. 200 holes for five farthings." Mr. Asquith, in his reply, said:

"There are

"I listened with the greatest interest to the statement read by Mrs. Scurr, and to what I may call the special individual experiences of the members of the deputation, by which that statement has been re-enforced. It has been a very moderate and well-reasoned presentation of your case, and I assure you I will give it very careful and mature consideration. I think I am right in saying that in substance the case you have presented to me comes to this—that the economic conditions under which women labour in a community, like, for instance, the East .End of London, are such that neither in the way of legislation, nor, perhaps, in the way of administration, can we get substantial and intelligent reform unless women themselves have a voice in choosing their representatives for Parliaments. You have given, each of you, special illustrations from your own experience, to show that it is not merely a rhetorical state- ment, but corresponds to the actual facts of East End life. I think it is only fair to Point out that these conditions, which are deplorable in many respects, have been substantially miti- gated I should like to see the intro- duction of women into the factory inspectorate in increasing numbers. But if everY woman over 21 had the vote and could exercise it in re- turning to the House of Commons people in sympathy with her wishes, you would still find the legislative problem excessively difficult. On one point I am in complete agreement with you. If the change has to come we must face it boldly, and make it thoroughly democratic in its basis."

Mr. Lansbury then pointed out that Miss Sylvia Pankhurst and Mrs. Walker were being punished for speeches and not for any form of violence. Mr. Asquith said: "I only want to know the facts; at least, that is the fact you presented to me, and your suggestion is that there is a different measure of justice or injustice meted out as between men and women in these matters, and next, quite apart from that alle- gation, your suggestion is that this is not an offence, such as any form of criminal violence would be, for which that would be an appropri- .

ate punishment. I will gladly speak to the • Home Secretary about these cases and consult him on the matter."

A WIDNES BABY'S DEATH.

Coroner Brighouse, commenting on a case before a Widnes jury, on Monday, of a child's death due to burning, said to the mother :—" I am not surprised! Are you? You are cleaning up the fireplace and you allow your baby (one year and ten months old) to carry away ashes on a. shovel from the fireplace." The dead child was Frank Burgess, the infant son of Elizabeth Burgess, of Castle-street, Wid- nes.—The mother said that she was cleaning the kitchen. She took away some cinders from the fire, and the child did likewise. She left the child in the yard, being called away for a few minutes. When she returned the child was on fire. She extinguished the flames and hurried away to the hospital. The child died next day from severe burns on the arms, legs and stomach.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," but added that with more care on the part of the mother the accident might have been avoided. Usk

WOMEN LIBERALS. Local Delegates at the London

Federation. MUSS CROSFIELD'S SURVEY.

June th.ei 9th, 10th and 11th, 1914, saw perhaps the finest Council meetings that the Women's Liberal Federation have yet held. The weather was good, the Queen's Hall spacious and its chairs comfortable; the speaking was of a high order throughout, and, what is more, mostly audible; and last, but not least, the varied dresses of the Executive and their stylish hats made the platform a pleasant thing to gaze upon! -

MRS. MACLAFIEN'S RETIREMENT.

The feature of the first day's meeting was, of course, the retirement of Mrs. Eva Maclaren from the post of treasurer to the Women's Liberal Federation, which she has held for 27 years. Loud as was the cheering for our aged President, .it paled before the storm of love and cathusiasm which thundered through the ball a full five minutes and more when the slender ,figure in grey stood up and essayed in vain to get a hearing. Bouquet after bouquet of ex- quisite pink, red and crimson dowers, varied with purple iris and yellow tea roses, were brought and laid in a row at her feet. "1 have been with the Federation since its start," said Mrs. Maclaren ; "only two others and myself now remain of the founders. And I am only leaving my post of treasurer, because I feel I can better serve Liberalism by concen- trating myself on the forward Suffrage policy at present. A rumour has been circulated that lam leaving the Federation. It is not true. I am an enemy to nothing but falseness of principles: I wish to uphold Liberal principles as against party expediency; and I shall only be down in the hall with the delegates, instead of up on the platform with the Executive." The Countess of Carlisle and Mrs. Eva ex- changed a kiss when these two out of the three remaining original members met on the plat- form. The President referred in moving terms to Mrs. Eva's retirement, and also to that of Mrs. Heron Maxwell, Mrs. Era's friend, "an untiring, loyal comrade, which is worth every- thing in life."

WOMAN'S WORLD.

salary of £250, suggests that dentistry is rich with opportunities as a cureer for women. In America the woman dentist and qualified assist- ant to a dentist, are successfully established, but very few educated women have taken up the pro- fession in this country. No doubt the time and expense of study and training to obtain the diploma, involve considerable sacrifices, and a course of instruction and practical 'work in a dental hospital, or the dental department of a general hospital, is necessary. A list of the sub- jects and examinations accepted by the Royal College of Surgeons may be obtained from the Registrar, 299, Oxford-street, London, W., and once the qualification is obtained it is quite easy for the woman dentist to secure a good and lucrative appointment.

straw hat, intended for wear with a white sum- mer frock of delicate lace and net. Many of these roses have each petal veiled in tulle, which heightens the soft effect. Very pretty are the wide-brimmed net hats in palest shades of pink, blue, or tele-de-negre, trimmed with roses, and supplied with ribbon velvet strings. Also the hat that has a flower crown of some large bloom, such as clematis, and a white muslin brim em- broidered in scallops to match the flowers in colour. All these are suitable for garden parties and summer functions generally. Tulle in pretty shades, rucked, tucked„ and ruched, makes light and pretty summer hats, and the amateur would experience no difficulty in " creating" them.

Pockets and Picot Edging. Pockets have made their appearance on some of the tailored skirts, although they are of no practical value. They are seen on waistcoats also, but are large enough only to hold a tiny handkerchief. Perhaps, later on, designers will find some place in which to put a really service- able receptacle for some of the impedimenta now carried in bags. Picot edging makes a very nice finish and is used on everything just now, from hat trimmings to underwear. For an edging on fine dress fabrics, tiny loops of cotton can be bought by the yard. A special appliance fitted to a sewing machine makes it very rapidly,, and some shops undertake to create it on materials bought by their customers. For underwear, which just now is quite devoid of frills and fus- siness, the picot edge is a particularly neat and suitable finish.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Rose in Ancient Times. It was a happy thought of. Queen Alexandra to dedicate our premier national emblem—the rose—to the sacred cause of charity, and the Rose Day festival in London this year was in every way a great success. In ancient times this flower was far more closely associated with every event, military, national, social and domestic, in the lives of the people, than it is with us to- day. Roses were carried by soldiers during the triumph of Roman generals, and the images of the gods, the altars, and temples, were deco- rated with rose garlands at all the great festi- vals. The Roman bride carried a wreath of roses and myrtle under her purple veil, afid the guests at banquets wore chaplets of these flowers on their heads, while wreaths of them were placed on the heads of the dead. Mourners were' permitted by the sumptuary laws of Rome to carry roses at funerals, and rose trees were planted on the graves of deceased persons. Soldiers often saved their pay to buy rose trees, which they planted in commemoration of their victories.

The Queen Mary Hostel. It is quite as it should be that the University which first granted degrees to women, should also be the first to inaugurate a department of Domestic Science, which will have a far-reach- ing effect on domestic labour and the work of the home. A diploma is to be instituted, and the work of the department cannot fail to raise the status of domestic duties and to benefit in- directly the national health. The hostel to which Queen Mary gave her name has now been opened as a residence for women students.

Women Dentists. The medical, teaching and nursing professions are all more or less concerned over the dearth of students. White and Black Preferred to COIOWS.

This year is remarkable for the lack of colour in the most fashionable frocks. To judge from the number of white dresses worn at modish carnivals, these will predominate for the re- mainder of, the season. White with black, as well as all black, are very noticeable, and most curious combinations of materials—or what would have been considered curious some years ago—are to be seen. Such, for instance, as white embroidered lawn with black satin, and tulle; but so cleverly combined that the effect is excel- lent. Sleeveless coatees of black tulle are worn over fine white linen tunics, the latter scalloped and embroidered, in handsome broderie anglaise. The cape' has been worn in some form by every other woman, and ought to be welcomed by the middle:aged, who hesitate to go out in their "figures," as the Americans say. Not that it is worn only by the older women, the youngest don it, and in many instances most successfully. The bolero-fichu corsage is another suitable de- sign for those who are no longer young, and for whom this season's fashions are not altogether kind.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
The Crinoline Scare

The two most noted balls in Paris during the short season were -the crinoline ball, given by the Duchesse de Gramont, and the jewel ball, which took place at the residence of the Prin- cesse de Broglie. The first of these two func- tions is responsible for the recent crinoline scare that is regularly revived at longer or shorter intervals. Certain Parisian dressmakers are said to have made many attempts of late to quietly introduce the hooped skirt, and there is every reason to believe that wider skirts will be favoured in the autumn. They are, as a matter of fact, seen now; but modern conditions of life preclude the possibility of a return to the distended and inconvenient skirts of the early days of Queen Victoria's reign.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
HINTS ON HEALTH.

Dangers of Unwashed Fruits.

To 'cultivate health successfully we 'must be continually on the alert against filth of all kinds, whether it is in the air We breathe, or in the food that we eat. Many people are alto- gether too careless about the food that they take, and little realise the danger of contamina- tion from the use of unwashed fruit. If they would stop to consider the class of people who gather the fruit and handle it they would be more careful. All fresh fruit, such as apples, apricots, grapes, plums, cherries, yes, and strawberries, raspberries, and blackberries, too, should be carefully cleaned and thoroughly washed before being placed on the table, whether they are served fresh or stewed. Where the Danger Arises.

It is not only the people who handle the fruit that must be considered, but also the danger of the collection of dirt in the transit and .while standing exposed in the shops or on the streets, where both flies and dust abound. -There is no doubt but that many seemingly obscure infec- tions and diseases have been transmitted .through carelessness iin the' use of unwashed fruit. If small fruit such as strawberries or raspberries are badly bruised it is impossible to cleanse them properly, and the only safe way of preparing them for the table is by cooking. Germs on Apple Skins.

The chief objection to the skin of an apple is the danger of contamination on account of the germs that accumulate on the surface. If the public were more exacting in their demands, and refused to purchase fruit from, careless shopkeepers and greengrocers, proper attention would be given to the care of the fruit, and they would see that it is protected from dust and fly contamination." Good Health." -

===== 0002601_19140704_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
COOKERY CORNER.

Some Useful Recipes.

Nettle tea is a fine summer beverage. It is not much trouble to make a supply of this simple cooling drink about twice a week, and it only costs the price of sugar and lemon. Gather and wash a large bunch of nettles (the young tops are the best), and put them into a saucepan of boiling water; boil slowly for about 30 minutes. Strain off the liquor, add the juice of a lemon, and as much sugar as you wish. When cold it is ready for usc,, and a wineglass- ful may be taken twice.

Rhubarb and Ginger Jam. This is quite easily made, and the cost is next to nothing. To every pound of rhubarb allow 11b. preserving sugar and about lox. of crushed root ginger. Put the rhubarb, cut small, into the preserving pan, with the sugar. Let it come slowly to boiling point; add the ginger, and then boil up well.: If the rhubarb is. youly, 20 minutes will do; if the fruit is old, you lied better give it i-hour. Just before putting into pots, stir. in a little almond essence.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
Green Tomato Jam.

Here is a recipe that will be of use when tomatoes refuse to ripen. To 41b. tomatoes allow .2 lemons and 31b. sugar. Cut the toma- toes in quarters. Put the sugar into the pan with just enough water to melt it, and add the lemons, sliced very thin. Cook for a few minutes; add the tomatoes, and boil again till clear, and the tomatoes quite tender. Pour into pots, and when cold, tie down.

SUFFRAGE AND SUFFRAGE SUPPORTERS. But the business of our great Council has been dealt with elsewhere, and I must not refer to the tremendous excitement of that afternoon culminating in the division taken on the North St. Pancras resolution, moved by Miss Alison Garland in masterly style. It was only lost by 54 votes this time, out of some 900 delegates; whilst last year the Tonbridge Wells resolution of similar tenor was lost by a two-thirds major- ity. It cheered the hearts of all those of us who believe that Woman Suffrage is a Liberal principle, which no expediency finally evade. Still better news was to come, however. Be- fore the sitting rose a lady was seen Moving to the front of the platform, a sheet of paper in her hand. It was the hard-working Controller of the Ballot, and a breathless silence fell as we all listened to the results:— Mrs. Lloyd George, 644. Mrs. F. E. Acland, 625. And then in succession came all the, names of the true and tried friends of Women's Suffrage practically heading the poll. Many a heart was glad that evening to think that the dear old W.L.F. had kept so staunchly by the up- holders of its principles, even if the delegates had not quite the courage to pass the St. Pan- cras resolution. How my fellow-delegates beguiled their even- ing I know not; but we all had lunch together in Waring's, and enjoyed a ramble through those palatial premises, and a fleeting glimpse of the vivid blouses, and delicious costumes in Bourne and Hollingworth's windows near by.

Entertained by Miss

Crosfield.

At the invitation of Miss Crosfield, the members of the committee of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association—Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. Lindley, Miss Booth, Mrs. O'Brien, Mrs. Dutton, Mrs. Bunch, Mrs. T. Locker, Mrs. Pendlebury, Mrs. Sanders, Mrs. Percival—to- gether with 120 members of the Sewing Party, visited Bryn Eithin, Colwyn Bay. The party left Warrington at 9.5 a.m. and on arrival at Colwyn Bay were received by Miss Crosfield, who gave them a hearty welcome. Miss Short was absent through illness. The party proceeded at once to the beach. Arrangements had been made for those who wished to bathe, and after enjoying some time on the sands in brilliant sunshine, the mem- bers partook of lunch. Charabancs afterwards conveyed the party to Bryn Eithin, the palatial residence of Mrs. Crosfield. Roses, carnations and many varieties of flowers were in full bloom. The green banks, lawns and foliage, together with the fine view of the sea and valley, made a delightful picture; some members of the party climbed the hills, under the escort of a Norwegian guide, many walking round the grounds, some resting in shady nooks; others playing at bowls. A programme of sports was carried out, with the following results

Sack Race for Committee.-1, Mrs. Percival; 2, Mrs. T. Locker.

Sack Race for Members.-1, Mrs. Archer; 2, Mrs Davies; 3, Mrs. Carter.

Potato Race.-1, Mrs. Davies; 2, Mrs. Swin- dell; 3, Mrs. Billington. Three-legged Race.—l, Mrs. Archer and Mrs. Stringer; 2, Mrs. Bibby and Mrs. Fogg. TO% was served in the grounds and everyone did full justice to the good things provided. After tea Miss ' Crosfield presented prizes, kindly provided by her, to the winners. On behalf of the members of the Sewing party, Mrs. VOSE presented Miss Crosfield with a handsome bag. She referred in glowing terms to the admiration they all felt for Miss Crosfield, who did so ;much to further their work in Warrington. Such a day as that would give them encouragement to go on working. Mrs. RIDGWAY then asked Miss Crosfield to accept a writing case from the members for Mrs. Crosfield, whose absence was regretted. It was given as a small token of affection for lofrs. Crosfield, who had interested herself in the work and welfare of the Sewing Class from its inauguration. They were all sorry that she was not able to be with them, because she would have been pleased to hear of the good work that was being done by the members for the cause of Liberalism. They wanted to make the association strong, and she hoped that every member would work hard to swell the ranks of the association. Miss Cuosrmi.o, in reply, said it was too good of them to think of her like that. She scarcely knew how to thank them for the beautiful bag, and also for the writing case for Mrs. Crosfield, who woald be very sorry to have missed the events of that day, and who would be delighted with the present. It had been a real pleasure to have them all there, and to see them all Bo

MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Diarrlicea Arnongst Infants.

This is a disease particularly fatal to children between three months and two years of age. It rarely attacks .breast-fed babies, and the cases are always more numerous during a hot summer than when the season is cool and wet. It is also more common in cities than in the country, due probably to infection being easier spread, where people are crowded.

- . In most cases the infection is carried by milk being impregnated with the germs, either by being kept in dirty utensils or contaminated by flies. The attack usuallY commences with vomiting, soon followed by a watery diarrhcea. The tongue is thickly coated, and there is at great thirst and restlessness. Unless steps are taken to immediately arrest the course of the disease it must end fatally.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
Treatment.

All milk must be discontinued, and the child be given nothing but white of egg, mixed with double the quantity of cold water; only tea- spoonfuls to be given at a time. Even this may not be retained by the stomach. Medical advice should be called in immediately, as every minute is of value in the treatment of the dis- ease.

If, however, the doctor cannot be obtained at once, give a dose of castor oil as soon as the diarrhoea commences, in order to clear away all irritating or decomposing food from the intes- tines. When the vomiting, is checked, a half- pint of strained barley water may be added to the white of egg mixture, but no milk must be given until all signs of the diarrhcea disappear, and then the milk must be well sterilised.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
LYNCH LAW AT ST. HELENS.

Lynch law was put in operation at St. Helens, ' as was stated at the local Court on Monday, when Robert Smith, a labeurer, of 1, Black Horse-road, exhibited evidences of very rough handling. He was charged with indecently assaulting little girls.—The Chief Constable said the offence was alleged to have taken place on Saturday afternoon near the chemical waste heaps by the side of the canal near Park-road. Two children, aged seven and six, were the com- plainants in the case. A woman saw the man with them, and she alled two men named Kinsey and Tunstall, who, after watching him for some time, set on to him and beat him severely, cutting and blackening his face extensively. After dealing with him on the principles of lynch law they handed him over to the police, to whom he stoutly denied that he had done anything wrong. He was sent to gaol for two months, and the magistrates complimented the men who gave him Zthrashing.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
A BRAVE ST. HELENS MAN.

The Lieutenant Foote, of H.M.S. Blonde, who was reported iu a cablegram from , St. Peters- burg as having been presented by the Czar with a gold medal for his gallantry in saving peasants from drowning at Reval, is Lieutenant A. G. Foote, a son of Alderman Foote, of Denton's Green, an ex-Mayor of St. Helens. Lieutenant Foote, after being educated at Cowley Schools, proceeded to the training ship Conway for edu- cation for the merchant service. He, however, succeeded in winning the King's gold medal in 1905, and was thus enabled to go into the Navy. As a cadet on the Conway, Lieutenant Foote also took the Trinity House prize, the M.S.S.A. gold medal, and the Parker prize.

CRICKET.

WARRINGTON CLUB IN INS. Hannam's Benefit Match.

The Warrington senior team was again vic- torious when at home on Saturday, and the match was given to Hannam, the professional, as his benefit. The home team was the first to occupy the crease, which they vacated when their aggregate score had reached 120. Hannam, the highest scorer of the day, was ten short of the half-century when P. Ball dis- missed him. He played remarkably well, as did W. Richardson, who had made 33 before he was caught by W. Gray. The visitors showed signs at the outset of surpassing the Warrington score, but towards the end the wickets fell frequently, and St. Helens Recs. could only muster 99, the chief contributor to which was G. Parry, who, with 30 not out, was the third highest scorer of the match. S. Partridge was in better form with the ball than with the bat, for his bowling average showed the capture of eight wickets at the cost of 29 runs. The professional failed to secure a single wicket, although his attempt was respon- sible for 30 run. W. Bennett bagged the re- maining two wickets for a couple of dozen runs, whilst G. Woodcock's fruitless endeavours cost nine runs. For St. Helens Rees..Tabern secured a single wicket for six runs, and P. Ball two for nine. The other wickets were obtained by S. Denton (four) and I'. Norbury (three), at the cost of 46 and 58 runs respectively.

Follow- ing the dismissal of J. Thompson, who was caught when he had made three, J. Bradburn and the Rev. J. M. The remainder of the team found it impossible to obtain double figures, and War- rington were all oat for 127.

SANKEY'S _VICTORY.

Sankey were successful when they visited the headquarters of the Moore and Daresbury eleven on-Satnrday, and secured victory by 71 runs against 47. Floyd, for the visitors, was the highest scorer with his 28, and Beddall, for the Cheshire team, was the gecond highest scorer with 19 runs.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
A CLOSE MATCH.

There was great excitement at the conclusion of the match between 80ld-street Wesleyans and Rylands' Recs. second eleven, for when the last wicket fell only one run separated the teams, the scores being 92-91, in favour of the Wesleyans, for whom Westin-ell and Davey obtained 36 and 33 runs respectively.. O'Brien was the to scorer for Rylands' Recs. with 35 to his credit.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET'S FINE WIN.

•At Arpley on Saturday, Bold-street Wesleyans defeated Cairo-street 2nd team by 56 to 19 runs. The Unitarians 'thus paid the penalty for field- ing a weak team against their doughty oppo- nents. For the winners, Westwell (17) and D. Weaver (13 not out) gave bright displays, espe- cially the latter, whose leg strokes were beauti- fully timed. Stringer (5 for 4) and Cooper (4 for 9) played havoc with the Unitarians, Mather being the only, player to Offer any resistance. Cooper per- formed the "hat trick." Ellsmore and Mather were the successful bowlers for Cairo-street, taking 6 for 18 and 4' for 6 respectively.

CROSFIELD'S (PRINTING DEPARTMENT)

DEFEAT STOCKTON HEATH 2nd _ _ •

_ Crosfield's Printers entertained Stockton Heath 2nd at Arpley on Saturday, when they dismissed them for 19 rumragairtet-62 scored IT the home team. Thompson (for Stockton Heath) and Barlow and Miller (for Crosfield's) did good work with the ball.

WATER POLO.

WARRINGTON v. BIRKENHEAD.

Warrington visited Birkenhead on Monday, but could not hold their opponents, who, defend- ing the shallow end, led by five goals to nil at the interval. In the second half H. Sisson and C. Turncr (captain) scored for Warrington, and Birken- head added two more through Gerstenkon, one of the fastest swimmers in the country. The final was: Birkenhead, 7 goals; Warrington, two goals. The Warrington team was not fully repre- sente4 S. Ince being indisposed, whereas their opponents turned out in full strength. C. Howarth, the Cheshire county player, appeared in goal, and in a great measure he was respon- sible for his side winning. Warrington meet Runcorn away on Friday nest, and in the second round for the Lanca- shire Junior Cup they play Accrington away on July 13th. Several of the old players, including T. S. Pemberton, have signed on again thia season.

MERSEY. CHAMPIONSHIP.

Over a new course from Dingle Point to a steamer moored near the training ships at Rock Ferry, the 36th annual championship of the Mersey took place on Saturday. H. Taylor (Hyde Seal) retained the championship, with time 20min. S. Roberts (Warrington) came in ninth, with time 26min. 40see.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
The Ex-Unionist Statesman Dies in London.

TWO BY-ELECTIONS PROBABLE.

Mr. J. Chamberlain, M.P. for West Birming- ham, and the ex-Unionist leader, died some- what unexpectedly on Thursday night in London. The news of his demise did not become known until yesterday morning, and it created a painful sensation amongst the de- ceased's friends in both political parties. The news was conveyed to the King, who was on friendly terms with the deceased, late on Thursday night.

Chamberlain's name will go down to posterity like those of Gladstone, Disraeli, and Pitt. M. Joseph Chamberlain was a business man first, and his political life was in accord with his early training. His school days were not lengthy. At the age of 16 he began his business career. This was in 1852, and for two years be went to his father's warehouse in London, learning, not only something of the routine of the counting house, but also of the actual mak- ing of boots and shoes, and working side by side with the men.

At the end of those two years his connection with the industry came to an end. He went down to Birmingham in 1854 to join his cousin Joseph Nettlefold in working a new patent in the manufacture of wood screw, and in extend- ing the operations of the firm of Nettlefold and Chamberlain.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0276.txt | included=true | included =====
ENTERED PUBLIC LIFE.

In 1865 the firm acquired other interests; the business grew enormously, and nine years later Joseph Chamberlain and three of his brothers retired. Roughly speaking, he had made his fortune in ten years. It was in 1865 that Mr. Chamberlain joined the Liberal Association, which was at that time agitating in favour of Mr. Gladstone's Reform Bill. The future states- man entered into politics as fervently and en- thusiastically as he conducted his business, and in 1867 he made his first important speech. In 1869 he first entered the Birmingham Town Council.

Chamberlain's election to the mayoralty of the town some time later created a sensation. He was a Radical of the ultra-republican type, and easy-going townspeople foresaw revolution. He did create a revolution, but it was not of the sort the timorous ones expected, and now- adays the city is thankful for his services to the municipality of which he was thrice mayor. However, when he entered Parliament in 1876, Mr. Chamberlain's fame had preceded him, and the grave old Yeomen who search the vaults before the opening of each new session ferreted and sought with special care below stairs. But he produced no bombs, hurled no burning brands. Indeed, his first speech was so admir- able in substance and tone that the "Times" devoted an amiable leading article to it. In a hop, stride and a jump Mr. Chamberlain was in the Cabinet under Gladstone, and old hands regarded him as a future Prime Minister.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0277.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME RULE SPLIT.

_ It was in 1886 that Mr. Chamberlain took the first step which marred his political career. He burst up the Liberal party on the question of IHome Rule, and from then was a queer mixture of Liberalism and rank Toryism. He was Colonial Secretary during the Boer War, and was chief engineer of the Khaki election in 1900. Three years later he sprang his Tariff Reform bombshell, and from then his political career practically ended. In July, 1907, he suffered from a stroke, and has been, despite statements to the contrary, in a precarious state of health.

TWO BY-ELECTIONS PROBABLE.. The death of Mr. J. Chamberlain will occa- sion a by-election in West Birmingham. The Unionist candidate will probably be Mr. Austen Chamberlain, the deceased's eldest son. The Labour party are to run Mr. lineeshaw, a prominent member of the 1.L.P., and it was announced some weeks ago that there was little likelihood of the Liberals running a candidate, thus leaving a free field for a fight between Labour, which is strong in the division, and Toryism.

If Mr. Austen Chamberlain stands there will have to be a by-election in Ea A Worcestershire. where a Liberal candidate will be run.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0278.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT ATTRACTIONS.

Warrington audiences enjoy one of Mrs. F. G. Kimberley's plays, and " The Wild Girl of the Forest," from her pen, is drawing large audi- ences to the Royal Court Theatre this week. This romantic military drama is full of divert- ing incidents, sensational and humorous, and a strong appeal is made in a charmingly unor- thodox love interest. The Prince of Floralia is wrongly accused of having inherited unenviable traits from his august father, and after a rising of the people Maritza, a child of the people, with whom loris has fallen in love, discovers ' the prince as quite the antithesis of what he is supposed to be, and becomes the princess, after they have overcome all the obstacles put in their way of jealousy, passion and selfishness. Mr. J. Newton Cowling's company bring out all the features in realistic fashion. Milton C. Curtis is a capital loris and Miss Edith Rutland is a charmingly impetuous and brave Maritza. Ben Carr play it the part of the treacherous captain in commendable style, and Mr. Arthur Trail puts all the necessary loyalty in the part of Penzar. Miss Edith Evans' Princess of Flor- alia is excellent, and Freddie L. Edwards as Trillo and Miss Lyn Darlington as Mima supply a fund of witty humour which is most enjoy- able. . . .

"The Thief" Next Week. At the Royal Court Theatre next week *Mr. Frank Weathersby, by special arrangement with Sir George Alexander, brings his excellent com- pany in "The Thief," which is one of the greatest successes that Sir George has achieved during his long regime at the St. James' Theatre. rn this moving well constructed play fine opportunities are afforded for the artistes. The plot of "The Thief" is constructed on a very small incident, and it is marvellous the dramatic situations that are obtained. The famous sensational incidents in the bedroom scene in the second act, during which the dis- tracted husband makes his wife admit herself a thief and the painful confession that he in- sists on her making to their host and hostess in the morning to clear the young lover of the wife who has taken on himself the blame, form the climax of this interesting and excellent play.

===== 0002601_19140704_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
HIPPODROME FARE.

Two hours are crammed full of bright, enter- taining items at the Hippodrome this week, and patrons are not slow to appreciate all that the management are doing for them in getting the " cream" to Warrington. " Ferdie's Ma-in- law" is the title of a funny sketch presented in an admirable fashion by Arnold Richardson, supported by Mr. Charles North and Miss Amy Lyon. The audience is convulsed with mirth over the "freezing plot," and the production is one of the best laughter-provokers we have seen for some time. Gillin is a ventriloquist, and by that we mean he is the genuine article--clever, witty and exceedingly original in a bright enter- tainment. Miss Violet Gwynne's Vesper Vio- lets are a triol of expert musicians, whose pleasing numbers are rendered with artistic taste. Marriott Edgar pays a welcome return visit as the "genial duchess," and is respon- sible for many laughs and vociferous plaudits. The Stein Esthor Trio are experts at hand balancing and sundry daring acrobatics. Harold Heath is a breezy light comedian and dancer, and the musical act presented so ably by Ena Vine and Mark Brewer is indeed charming. Next Week's Programme. Frank Elliston and company will appear in " Three and a Fool" next week, and other turns will be: Four Millons, gymnastic won- ders; the Four Amaranths, sensational novelty dancers; Six Buster Browns and a piano; Edwin Lawrence, "Scroggy and Scout"; Three O'Con- nor Girls, musicians and club swingers; Tew and Lea, novelty comedians.

AUSTRALIA. The Governments of New South Wales and Victoria have arranged for their representative to attend from 4 to 9 p.m. on THURSDAY, JULY 2nd, At the WHEAT SHEAF HOTEL, WALGATE, WIGAN, and on SATURDAY NEXT, JULY 4th, At the - PATTLN ARMS HOTEL, PARKER- STREET, WARRINGTON, to -give free advice to intending settlers, and also to select men with farm- ing experience, boy learners, 16 to 21 years of age, and domestic servants. Boys need have no previous experience, but will be placed on farms at commencing wages of from 10s. to 15s. per week and board and lodgings. Experi- enced men from 20s. to 30s. per week. Domestics, 12s, 6cl. to 255. per week. All granted low steamer fare and part lent. Men and boys need only pay £3 here.; domestics £l. Letters should be sent to MESSRS. JOHN E. RIDGWAY, LTD., 3 Piccadilly, Manchester; or, 60, Fisherg,ate, Preston.

CIOUNTY .PALATINE OF CHESTER. TUBERCULOSIS ORDER OF 1914. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the , Board of Agriculture and Fisheries have by an Order dated the 23rd day of June, 1914, and which COMES INTO OPERATION ON TIES Ist DAY OF JULY, 1914, revoked, as and from the Ist day of July, 1914, the Tuberculosis Order of 1913, the purport and object of which has already been published by means of posters and handbills circulated amongst Farmers and Owners of Stock, and such new Order re-enacts the provisions of the said revoked Order with certain changes, the more important of which are as follows, viz.:— ARTICLE I.—INTERPRETATION. "Emaciation due to Tuberculosis" is through- out the New Order substituted for " Tubercu- losis with Emaciation." ARTICLE 2.—NOTICE OF DISEASE. • Notification is required of any bovine suffer- ing from a chronic cough, and showing definite clinical signs of tuberculosis. ARTICLE 4.—EXAMINATION OF ANIMALS. The application of the Tuberculin test is limited to Cows suspected of suffering from Tuberculosis of the Udder, or of giving tuber- culous milk, and to bovines suspected of suffer- ing from tuberculous emaciation. ARTICLE 6.—VALUATION FOR COMPEN- SATION. Only one Valuation has to be made, and that has to be the sum the animal would be worth in the open market in its actual condition, and not the sum the animal would be worth if it were fr3e from disease or suspicious symptoms. AIniCLE B.—COMPENSATION. The scale of compensation has not been altered, but the amount payable will be differ- ent, as it is now based on the market value. This applies t.% every •-ase of Ela3khi'er, on or after the date of operation of the new Order, whether the notice of intended slaughter .was given before or after that date. The provision requ-ring that the carcase of an animal that is emaciated, and in which tuberculous lesions are present, shall be considered as suffering from adt anced tuberculosis is omitted. ARTICLE 9. —PRECAUTIONS TO BE ADOPTED WITH RESPECT TO MILK. The restrictions as to the use of milk of a suspected Animal cease to apply after the ex- piration of three clear days from the date of the Veterinary examination of the Animal. They come into force again when notice of intended slaughter is served. Further particulars and details of the new Order can be obtained from the Police Officers of the County Constabulary appointed as In- spectors under the Contagious Diseases Animals Acts, and special notice is given to Owners of Bovine Animals of the following Article, viz.t— NOTICE OF DISEASE. 2.—(1) Every person having in his possession or under his charge (1) Any cow which is. or appears to be sfuffering from tuberculosis of the udder, in- durated udder, or other chronic disease of the udder; or (2) Any bovine animal which is, or appears to be, suffering from tuberculous emaciation; or (3) Any bovine animal which is suffering from a chronic cough and showing definite clinical signs of tuberculosis, shall without avoidable delay give information of the fact to a constable of the police force for Vol; oafr etahewih,oecraeilnthe Authority,anim l andis. tohretocoannstlanbAlpe ecor- Inspector shall transmit the information to the Local Authority. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk to the Cheshire County Council. County Offices, Northgate-street, Chester, 30th June, 1914.

A RUNCORN STAGE, MANAGER'S WAGES. A successful application on behalf of Thomas Ford, 5, St. Sohn-street, Runcorn, for the sum of 18s. Bd. wages due to him from Mr. Alfonso Smith, the proprietor of the Empress Assembly Hall, Lowland-road, was made at the local Court on Monday. Mr. Linaker said his client was engaged by Mr. Smith as stage manager and engineer at a salary of 28s. per week, and on the 6th inst. he was given a week's notice by the manager,but two days afterwards was sent away and was paid 9s. 4d. Mr. Smith did not appear. —The Bench granted the application, together with costs and advocate's fee. It was stated at an inquest at Leigh on Wil- liam Ashton, a collier, of Twist-lane, who com- mitted suicide in the canal, that he was worried about debt.

FOOTBALL FEATURES.

Warrington N.O Club Arrange an Attractive Fixture Card. LAWS CAPTURED FROM WIDNES. BY "SCRUM." It seemed somewhat ambiguous whilst one was sweltering under the heat wave of the present week to be suddenly brought into contact with the great winter pastime of football. The managers of the game, like the Christmas card makers, have, however, to look ahead, and mid- summer finds them preparing for winter. The fixtures for the Warrington N.U. Club have now been prepared, and I have been privileged to receive an advance copy. A very interesting programme has been devised , —one which ought to keep the excitement at top mark, and one which should produce some excel- lent games, both on the Wilderspool pitch and upon foreign grounds. As the committee are now assiduously endeavouring to strengthen the team, I hope to' see the club rise high in the competition. LEAGUE FIXTURES. The same' number of League fixtures as last season has been arranged, namely, 32. The

A WET WALKING DAY.

charge of Miss White (girls), and Mr. F. Stans- field (boys), and Mr. R. Gunner, chief sidesman, also accompanied the procession. There was a tableau representing an infantile Maypole group. ST. CLEMENT'S. St. Clement's scholars made a fine display. The Rev. T. Vickers, curate-in-charge, assisted by Mr. H. Houghton, representative sidesman, led nearly GOO children along the route. The band from the Liverpool Farm School was respon- sible for the music, and this school presented, an imposing picture. ST. PETER'S SCHOOL. Perhaps the most striking feature of all the procession was provided by the St. Peter's School, who mustered about 700 strong. The company was headed by the Rev. B. Percival, who was accompanied by his church officials and many of the teachers. A group of pretty little lasses, dressed to represent fisher girls, attracted immediate attention. Then came flower girls and a dainty Red Riding Hood.

There are still many people who buy spectacles as our grandfathers bought them fifty years ago. They patronize the door-to-door pedlar, or pick a pair from a tray exposed on some market stall. This is amazing when one thinks of the progress which has been made in the science of spectacle fitting. Our Grandfathers were blameless in this respect. They had no opportunity of doing differently. This cannot be said of their children. Have your eyes tested by Johnston Watson Sight Testing Optician, 84, BRIDGE ST. WARRINGTON. Steel Spectacles from 2/6. Rolled Gold from 8/6. THE VALUE OF TREES. Not so long ago the deadlkst foe of trees was the house builder. When the suburbs of Lon- 'don were extending at a rapid rate, say, twenty years ago, many a fair grove of trees was sacri- ficed .to make way for a row of brick villas with stucco ornaments. Nowadays this atti- tude has been changed. The poorest man. who builds a house recognises the value of having a tree near it, and the rich man carefully looks out for a site where the ground will have the appearance of maturity even when he enters it. It is an ambition with him either to be sur- rounded with trees or to have a plantation at his back.—" Country Life." 1 The names of Mr. William Stubbs, Belmont. Winsford, and Mr. Joseph Pybus Jackson, of Whitegate House, Northwich, agent to Lord Delamere, have been placed 04 the QuarAiUlgil of the Peace for Derbyagrji,

ANGLING. 'Warrington Workingmen's Associa- tion. N.A. CENTRE. There was not a great mister of members out last week-end, probably owing to the holidays this week. Several visited Chester, Mickle Trafford and Norton, and specimens have made their appearance, but they are of no great weight. Still, they are there to be beaten, and it is advisable that members should not miss any opportunity to weigh in, as last season specials went a-begging for the sake of members not making the most of their catches. THE GOWY. About twenty locals fished the Gowy last Sunday. There were no great catches regis- tered, and those who took fish found them in the shallows; nothing was done by the members for the Jl 31-eNlsi wasot Norton riN;fitit.aesoelknii geee t:atNsl_soz eehfl visitediaidtkehene eerep•edhwaoboyaf yly. e members. eTeini Arrangements are members sjnibees ereism.wes. One was weighed in scal- ing 6oz. 7dr.; this is the first to be put on the scales this year. However, as the canals up and down are doing so badly, roach of 6oz. must not I)€ discarded. We bear that the match at Patricroft was a blank in certain lengths of the competition, and several anglers, who are acknowledged to be the best in Lancashire, came away without having a stir for the whole of the two hours. great was done at Chester, either in tl.e Dee or canal, with the exception of W. Henshall's eel, which scaled 11b. 7oz. HEADQUARTERS SPECIALS, 1914. (Gladstone Arms). Now that the open season has arrived, we publish a list of specials for season 1914. The above specials are apart from the set board prizes given each season. If the present I season is financially a success a general meeting will be called to raise- the prizes on the set board to three, instead of two prizes, given at present. A RECORD. Details are now at hand concerning the fish caught at Ellesmere, and although perhaps not a record for roach in all England, it must be put down as a record for Warrington. Seven roach were taken in six.minutes, weighing over 71b. Sport was that good that the natives said they had never witnessed such a catch. Alto- gether the roach. forwarded to Warrington were fifty-one, and they weighed 60Ib. NOTICES.
